# Foreword

A pure Christian doctrine is, among all temporal gifts and blessings from God, perhaps the very greatest and most important, because without it, all other gifts of God would be of very little benefit to our true well-being. Through it, not only is the foundation for all good morality <note>original: *Sittsamkeit*—refers to a combination of character, decency, and ethical conduct</note> established, by which we may be useful and pleasing to God and our fellow human beings, and taught how we should use all of God's gifts for our salvation; but it also shows us most clearly how we may be rescued from all misery through the knowledge of God and our Savior <term>Savior: *Heilmacher*, literally 'Heal-maker' or restorer</term> Jesus Christ, and attain an eternal and supreme happiness. The Son of God himself and his apostles, driven and equipped by the power of the Holy Spirit, brought this doctrine to light and set it on its path, indicating that this good news of God should be faithfully offered and proclaimed to all people until the Last Day. All honest servants of Jesus Christ have faithfully applied themselves to this at all times, seeking to promote and spread it in every way possible.

Next to the preaching of the Gospel <note>original: *Evangelii*—the Latin genitive form for 'of the Gospel'</note>, through which people are generally invited to the Lord Jesus and the enjoyment of his benefits, the method of promoting Christian doctrine among the inexperienced and beginners through catechizing <term>catechizing: a traditional method of religious instruction using a series of questions and answers</term> has been considered by the wise to be the most useful and best. As one then ge-

<summary>This preface introduces the "pure Christian doctrine" as the most vital gift from God for achieving both earthly morality and eternal salvation. It specifically highlights catechization as the superior method for instructing beginners in the faith.</summary>
<keywords>Christian doctrine, salvation, Jesus Christ, Holy Spirit, Gospel, catechism, morality, instruction</keywords>

# Foreword

...[certain] and irreproachable testimonies from Church Histories show that in those congregations established by the Apostles themselves, certain notable persons were appointed to such service and produced very great benefits. At the time of the Reformation, the necessity of this method of teaching was recognized by almost all famous teachers and was very highly recommended and praised. One of them calls it:

> "the most 
> "necessary element, about which the Fathers wrote much, as it was always 
> "maintained in the early Church, wherein Christians should be 
> "raised, nurtured, and taught as much as possible from 
> "their youth."

And says, if the same were neglected:

> "how then would it be a wonder if 
> "henceforth there were few Christians."

(See **C. S. Epistolar, Part II**, Book **1**, page **368**. <note>C.S. likely refers to Caspar Schwenkfeld (1489–1561), a Reformation theologian; his *Epistolar* was a widely read collection of his letters and treatises.</note> Likewise, **Part I Epist.** at the front, page **XIV. XV.**)

Under the name **catechizing**, one generally understands that **method of teaching** where, through questions and answers, the **main doctrines of Christianity** are discussed and imparted to the **inexperienced** briefly, clearly, and thoroughly. **Catechizing** means in English <note>original: *auf Deutsch*—meaning the German language of the author</note>: to instruct someone orally with a loud voice through question and answer <note>The author is referring to the Greek root *katecheo*, which literally means "to sound down" or "to echo."</note>. And as it is used nowadays, it means: to instruct someone in the knowledge of the first and most necessary **fundamental truths of Christianity** through question and answer. **Paul** makes use of this word in his language <note>The author refers to the original Greek text of the New Testament.</note> in **Romans 2:18, 1 Corinthians 14:19**, and twice in **Galatians 6:6**, and **Luke** writes of **Apollos**:

<summary>This page defines "catechizing" as a foundational oral teaching method using questions and answers, tracing its use from the early Apostles through the Reformation.</summary>
<keywords>catechizing, Reformation, Apostles, Caspar Schwenkfeld, religious instruction, Paul the Apostle, Apollos, Church History</keywords>

<summary>This page argues that catechizing—the practice of oral, systematic religious instruction—is an ancient and universal method rooted in the Bible and the early Church Fathers like Chrysostom and Augustine. It emphasizes that this simple teaching style is historically the primary way to ground both children and adults in the fundamentals of faith.</summary>
<keywords>catechism, religious education, Apostolic Church, John Chrysostom, St. Augustine, Johann Jakob Rambach, biblical precedents</keywords>

# Preliminary Remembrance

(*Rambach’s Catechetics* <note>Johann Jakob Rambach (1693–1735) was a prominent Lutheran theologian and hymn writer; his works on "Catechetics" or the art of teaching the faith were highly influential.</note>, the introduction, *page 16.*) Furthermore, he says:
> " Regarding public catechizations <note>original: *Catechiſationes*</note>, 
> " the most necessary fundamental truths were 
> " presented therein clearly, simply, and in a 
> " childlike manner, and everything was 
> " taken from the pure source of Holy Scripture, 
> " so that the foundation of faith might 
> " be firm and unmovable. " *Chr. Starke* <note>Christoph Starke (1684–1744) was a German theologian famous for his "Synopsis," a comprehensive Bible commentary used by laypeople and clergy alike.</note>

in his *Synopsis* writes regarding *Luke 2:46.*
> " The 
> " method of teaching with questions and answers 
> " was sanctified by Christ; it is the 
> " oldest, most useful, and most 
> " edifying. " *Genesis 3:9. Matthew 22:41.*

From these testimonies and examples, it is sufficiently clear how necessary, useful, and obligatory the exercise of the catechism <note>original: *Catechismus-Uebung*</note> is, especially since ignorance is innate to us all, and yet is most unbecoming for a Christian; for it is the true door to all harmful errors and makes a person unfit for the worship, confession, and praise of God, for which God created and called mankind. Therefore, it is to be heartily wished that all Christian parents, who are commanded to bring up their children in the nurture and admonition of the Lord, *Ephesians 6:4*, would take this truth rightly to heart and, following the example of the dear old pious Christians, be earnestly mindful that their children and the dear youth as a whole should not lack such necessary instruction. They should also be mindful of and employ every conceivable way and means so that the poor youth might be encouraged—

# Preliminary Remembrance

...em, <note>This completes a word from the previous page, likely regarding the children's ability to "enjoy" or "relish" their lessons.</note> to gain a desire and love for instruction, and then at the same time to also heartily call upon GOD for His blessing upon it. If this were to happen, the world would undoubtedly soon be in a better state than it unfortunately appears to be, given the great neglect.

So that such Christian practice <note>original: *Christliche Uebung*</note> might be promoted more conveniently among our people in these times, these following short questions and answers have been brought together, in which the most necessary main parts <term>Haupt-Stücke: The core sections of the Lutheran catechism, traditionally including the Ten Commandments, the Creed, the Lord's Prayer, the Sacrament of Holy Baptism, Confession, and the Sacrament of the Altar.</term> and fundamental truths of the Christian religion are presented simply, briefly, and thoroughly, so that it may be a helpful guide to pious parents, as well as to those who are tasked with leading the practice of the catechism publicly, on how youth should be introduced to Christian doctrine through question and answer. In this collection, the work of previous authors has been used extensively, as will soon be noticed by those experienced in these matters. Everything is also richly supported with Holy Scripture, so that those who are already somewhat practiced can be shown immediately how the answer is grounded in Holy Scripture; in doing so, one can also find opportunity to form further questions from the scriptural text, so that the matter may be more thoroughly grasped and more deeply impressed upon the mind. 

The practice of confirming the teachings of the catechism with Holy Scripture was already in great use among the ancients <note>Referring to the Early Church Fathers of the first few centuries.</note>, as touched upon above. Cyril of Jerusalem <note>original: *Cyrillus Hierosol.* (c. 313–386 AD); a bishop and theologian famous for his "Catechetical Lectures" for new converts.</note>, the great catechist, addressed his catechism students thus: "You must not simply take my word for it immediately,"

## Preliminary Remembrance

"...when I put something forward; unless you receive a proof of it from the Holy Scripture." <note>original: "Heil. Schrift". This quote, likely continuing from the previous page, is a hallmark of the Protestant emphasis on "Sola Scriptura"—the idea that all religious teaching must be grounded in the Bible.</note>

It certainly requires much faithfulness, seriousness, and diligence if something truly useful is to be accomplished with the youth, since one must work upon and look to not only the children’s **understanding** but also simultaneously their **will**, so that both may be improved. For although knowledge is the primary thing we need, experience teaches us all too well that knowledge alone—without a love for the matter (which is the inclination of the will)—is of no use for our salvation; according to 2 Peter 2:21 and 1 Corinthians 8:1. <note>2 Peter 2:21 suggests it is better not to have known the way of righteousness than to know it and turn away; 1 Corinthians 8:1 famously states that "knowledge puffs up, but love builds up."</note> And because such love is one of the good gifts of the Holy Spirit, for which GOD desires to be petitioned—just as the illumination of the understanding toward a true knowledge <term>Erkenntniß: In this context, it refers to a deep, spiritual realization of truth rather than mere intellectual facts.</term> is also such a gift, which simultaneously presupposes and requires a childlike fear of God (which is the beginning of wisdom): it is therefore necessary that a heartfelt calling upon GOD be not set aside by either those teaching or those learning, but should rather be performed and carried out with great devotion.

That the gracious, almighty, eternal, and kind GOD—according to His gracious pleasure in Christ JESUS our Lord—might maintain His praise in us and in our dear youth as His human and dearly redeemed creatures; and that He might graciously allow His divine blessing to flow over this little work <note>original: "Werkgen". The author uses a diminutive form, a common sign of humility in 18th-century religious writing.</note> for the advancement of His salvific knowledge, is the heartfelt wish and prayer of His unworthy servant, the **Author.**

<summary>The author concludes the preface by arguing that religious education must transform both the mind and the heart, ending with a humble prayer for the book's success in guiding the youth.</summary>
<keywords>Christian education, intellect and will, Holy Spirit, fear of God, salvation, pedagogy, prayer, scriptural authority</keywords>

->A<-

# Short Instruction

->for<-

## Young Children.

1. **Who created us?**
GOD has created me, and all people.

2. **When did GOD create the first human?**
When GOD had created heaven and earth, and everything that is within them, in six days, He finally, on the sixth day, also made the human being.

3. **What was the first human called?**
Adam.

4. **What was the first human’s state like?**
GOD created the human in His <term>image</term> <note>original: "Bilde". Referring to the "Imago Dei," the concept that humanity was created to reflect God's character and nature.</note>, and placed him in a <term>state of innocence</term> <note>original: "Stand der Unschuld". In theology, this describes the original condition of humanity before the introduction of sin.</note>.

5. **What kind of authority did GOD give to the human?**
He made him lord over everything He had made.

<vocab>GOD, Human, Adam, Creation, Innocence, Instruction</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the basic tenets of creation to young students, identifying God as the Creator and Adam as the first human, originally made in God's image and given authority over the world.</summary>
<keywords>Creation, Adam, Imago Dei, state of innocence, religious education, children's catechism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catechism for children, moving from the creation of humanity to the Fall of Man and the promise of a Savior. It follows logically from Question 5 on the previous page regarding man's original authority over creation.</meta>

<header>Short Instruction</header>

->6. What else did GOD ordain for him<-
->in this state?<-

He gave him commandments and teaching regarding what he should do and what he should avoid.

->7. Did he also remain in this state?<-
->No: he has fallen.<-

->8. How then did he come to such a<-
->fall?<-

Through his disobedience toward GOD; by eating of the forbidden fruit.

->9. What did this fall bring about?<-

<margin>Genesis 2:17.</margin>
It brought all misery <note>original: "Dürftigkeit". In this context, it refers to the state of human frailty, need, and physical/spiritual deprivation resulting from sin.</note> and death upon himself, and upon us, all his descendants.

->10. What then did GOD do after such a<-
->fall into sin?<-

He brought him before judgment, showed him his condemnation, and promised him a <term>Redeemer</term> <note>original: "Erlöser". A theological term for one who pays a price to "buy back" or save humanity from the consequences of sin.</note>.

->11. Has this promised<-
->Redeemer also come?<-

<margin>Galatians 4:4.</margin>
Yes: GOD sent Him in the <term>fullness of time</term> <note>original: "Völle der Zeit". A biblical phrase referring to the exact moment in history chosen by God for the birth of Christ.</note>.

->12. Who then was this Redeemer?<-

It was the <term>only-begotten</term> <note>original: "eingeborne". Meaning the unique, one-of-a-kind Son.</note> Son of GOD, named Jesus Christus.

->13. Has the Son of GOD redeemed you?<-

Yes: The only-begotten Son of GOD, Jesus Christus, GOD and man, is my Redeemer.

->14. By what means has the Son<-
->of GOD redeemed us?<-

By fulfilling the law of GOD in our place through a perfect obedience;

[fulfil]led; and that he paid our debt of sin with his blood and death on the cross.
->Hebrews 10:7, 10.<-

15. What has Christ obtained for us through his obedience, suffering, and death?
The grace of GOD, the Holy Spirit, and Eternal Life.

16. What good does the grace of GOD do for us first?
It convinces us of our misery of sin, and how necessary <term>sanctification</term> <note>original: "Heiligung". This refers to the process of being made holy or being changed by God to live a more moral and spiritual life.</note> is for us; and it points us to our <term>Redeemer</term>.

->17. Who sanctifies us?<-
The Holy Spirit, the Lord and Giver of Life.

->18. How does the Holy Spirit act<-
->when he wants to sanctify us?<-
He teaches us to recognize our sin, to repent of it with a penitent heart and hate it; and how we should take hold of Jesus Christ and his <term>righteousness</term> <note>original: "Gerechtigkeit". In a religious sense, this means being made "right" or "just" in the eyes of God through faith.</note> through faith, as well as how we should be renewed in him. 1 Corinthians 6:11.

->19. But what must we do so that<-
->we may be sanctified?<-
We must be obedient to this teaching of the Holy Spirit; and also live a modest, righteous, and godly life; and avoid all ungodly nature and sin.

->20. What is sin?<-
It is disobedience against GOD's commandment.

->21. What kind of temptations to<-
->sin do we have?<-
There are especially three of them.

<meta>This page continues the "Short Instruction" or catechism for young children from page 3. The previous page ended by asking about the "three temptations to sin." This page begins by identifying those temptations and then moves into the basics of Christian practice: prayer and the profession of faith.</meta>

<header>Short Instruction.</header>

22. Which are they?
They are the desires of our flesh, the allurements of the world, and the snares of the devil. <note>In this context, "flesh" (original: "Fleisches") refers to human nature's inclination toward selfishness or sin rather than physical skin.</note>

23. How can one escape them?
Through prayer and watching. <note>The term "watching" (original: "Wachen") refers to spiritual alertness or being on guard against temptation.</note>

24. What is prayer?
It is a lifting up of the mind and soul to GOD. <note>original: "Erhebung des Gemüths." The "Gemüth" refers to the inner self, including thoughts and emotions.</note>

25. Can you also say any prayer?
Yes: That which our Savior, Christ, taught his disciples; which every pious child should learn by heart.

26. Let me hear it?
Our Father, who art in heaven. Hallowed be thy name. Thy kingdom come. Thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven. Give us this day our daily bread. And forgive us our debts, as we forgive our debtors. And lead us not into temptation; but deliver us from evil. For thine is the kingdom, and the power, and the glory, from eternity to eternity, Amen.

27. My dear child, that is quite right:
But is this all that you know?
No: I can also say the <term>Creed</term> <note>original: "den Glauben," literally "the faith." In a catechism, this specifically refers to the Apostles' Creed, a summary of Christian beliefs.</note>.

28. What is the Creed that you can say?
It is a confession of those things which I believe are necessary for my salvation.

29. Now, how does the Creed go?
I believe in GOD, the Father, Almighty Creator of heaven and earth.
And in JESUS Christ, his only-begotten
->Son,<-

<header>for Young Children.</header>

Son, our Lord. Who was conceived by the Holy Spirit, born of the Virgin Mary. Suffered under Pontius Pilate, was crucified, died, and was buried. He descended into hell, on the third day he rose again from the dead. He ascended into heaven, and sits at the right hand of GOD, the Father Almighty. From thence he shall come to judge the living and the dead.

I believe in the Holy Spirit. A holy Christian Church, the communion of saints. The forgiveness of sins. The resurrection of the body. And an eternal life. Amen. <note>This completes the Apostles' Creed, the foundational statement of Christian belief used in many denominations since the early centuries of the church.</note>

30. That is also right: But is nothing more required of us than praying and believing?

->Yes: We should also take diligent care,<-
that we keep all the holy commandments of GOD.

### 31. Which are the commandments of GOD?

The commandments of GOD we find described in the Old and New Testament.

### 32. What is the sum of the commandments in the New Testament?

The main sum <note>original: "Summa," a Latin term meaning the total amount or the essence of a matter.</note> of the commandment is: Love from a pure heart, and from a good conscience, and from a sincere faith. <margin>1 Timothy 1:5.</margin> <note>original: "ungefärbtem Glauben," literally "uncolored" or "undisguised" faith, meaning a faith that is genuine and not just for show.</note>

### 33. What is the sum of the commandments in the Old Testament?

They are the holy Ten Commandments.

### 34. Where do the Ten Commandments come from?

GOD wrote them with his own hand onto two stone tablets, and gave them to Moses. <note>This refers to the biblical account in the Book of Exodus, where Moses receives the law on Mount Sinai.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the recitation of the Apostles' Creed and introduces the Ten Commandments as the next essential part of a child's religious education.</summary>
<keywords>Apostles' Creed, Ten Commandments, New Testament, Old Testament, 1 Timothy 1, Moses, Christian education</keywords>

<header>Short Instruction for Young Children.</header>

<meta>catchword: I. From</meta>

35. I would also like to hear those same Ten Commandments from you?

<margin>I.</margin> The First Commandment: You shall have no other gods before me.

<margin>II.</margin> The Second: You shall not make for yourself any idol <note>original: "Bildniß," meaning a carved image or representation</note>, nor any likeness. Do not worship them, and do not serve them.

<margin>III.</margin> The Third: You shall not misuse the name of the LORD your GOD.

<margin>IV.</margin> The Fourth: You shall keep the holy day sacred. <note>original: "Feyertag," referring here to the Sabbath or day of rest.</note>

<margin>V.</margin> The Fifth: You shall honor your father and your mother, so that it may go well with you, and you may live long upon the earth.

<margin>VI.</margin> The Sixth: You shall not kill.

<margin>VII.</margin> The Seventh: You shall not commit adultery.

<margin>VIII.</margin> The Eighth: You shall not steal.

<margin>IX.</margin> The Ninth: You shall not bear false witness against your neighbor.

<margin>X.</margin> The Tenth: You shall not covet your neighbor’s house, wife, manservant, maidservant, cattle, or anything that is his.

36. What does GOD now say of all these commandments?

He says thus: I the LORD, your GOD, am a strong, jealous <note>original: "eiferiger," which can mean zealous or jealous, expressing God's protective demand for exclusive devotion</note> GOD, who visits the iniquity <note>original: "Missethat," a sin or wrongful deed</note> of the fathers upon the children unto the third and fourth generation <note>original: "Glied," literally "limb" or "link," used here to mean a degree of lineage</note> of those who hate me: and show mercy unto many thousands of those who love me and keep my commandments.

-> Exodus 20, 5. 6. <- <note>original: "2 Mos.," refers to the Second Book of Moses, known in English as the Book of Exodus.</note>

<term>Ten Commandments</term> (the Decalogue; the fundamental laws of morality and worship), <term>Catechism</term> (a summary of religious doctrine used for instruction), <term>GOD</term>, <term>Neighbor</term> (one's fellow human being), <term>Iniquity</term> (sin or wrongful act), <term>Mercy</term> (compassion or forgiveness), <term>Exodus 20</term> (the biblical chapter containing the Ten Commandments).

# I. Of the Divine Essence,
## and Its Attributes.

1. Do you believe that there is a God?
Yes, I believe that there is one single <note>original: "einiger," emphasizing both the unity and uniqueness of God</note> true God. Mark 12:29.
2. Is there then more than one God?
No, there is only One God. Mark 12:32.
1 Cor. 8:4.
3. Who is God?
God is a Spirit. John 4:24.
4. What kind of Spirit is God?
He is an eternal, independent <note>original: "selbständiger," meaning self-existent or existing by His own power</note>, omnipresent, and perfect Spirit. Gen. 21:33.
Ps. 139:7ff. <note>original: "seq.," an abbreviation for the Latin *sequens*, meaning the following verses</note>
5. Does He then also have understanding and will?
Certainly; His understanding is unsearchable, and 
He can create whatever He wills. Isa. 40:28.
Ps. 115:3.
6. Which are the most prominent <note>original: "vornehmsten," meaning chief or most excellent</note> attributes of the divine understanding?
Omniscience and wisdom. Dan. 2:20.
7. Which are the most prominent attributes of His will?

<note>The vocabulary below corresponds to the key terms used in this lesson on the nature of God.</note>
<term>divine essence</term>, <term>attributes</term>, <term>spirit</term>, <term>understanding</term>, <term>will</term>, <term>omniscience</term>, <term>wisdom</term>, <term>God</term>

<summary>This page introduces the first chapter of a children's catechism, defining God as a single, eternal, and perfect Spirit characterized by infinite wisdom and a sovereign will.</summary>
<keywords>divine essence, attributes of God, monotheism, Holy Spirit, omniscience, divine wisdom, catechism for children</keywords>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>Of the Divine Essence.</header>
<meta>catchword: 15. How</meta>

Love, justice, and omnipotence. <note>These three words complete the answer to question 7 from the previous page regarding the attributes of God's will.</note>
8. What is this God called?
He is called: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.
Matthew 28:19.
9. Are then Father, Son, and Holy Spirit not three Gods?
No, they are indeed three equal and distinct <note>original: "unterschiedene," meaning they are not merely different names for the same thing, but distinct in their relations</note> Persons, yet they are one in essence and thus only One God. 1 John 5:7. John 10:30.
10. What is a Person?
A "Person" is the name given to such a living being that has understanding and a free will; an indivisible whole <note>original: "unmittheilbar Ganzes," a technical term from scholastic theology meaning a "subsistent individual" that cannot be shared or split into parts</note> in which no other can have a part.
11. How can you prove the distinction of the Persons in God?
The Son says: I will pray to the Father, and He shall give you another Comforter <note>original: "Tröster," referring to the Holy Spirit</note>.
John 14:16, 26. Matthew 28:19.
12. By what is the first Person distinguished from the others?
By the fact that He is a Father and has begotten a Son. Romans 15:6. Hebrews 1:5.
<note>The following question is numbered 12 again in the original text, likely a printer's error for 13.</note>
12. By what is the second Person distinguished from the Father and the Holy Spirit?
By the fact that He is a Son who was begotten of the Father. John 1:18. Colossians 1:15.
14. By what is the third Person distinguished from the Father and the Son?
By the fact that He is the Spirit of the Father and the Son, who proceeds <note>original: "ausgehet," the theological term for the Holy Spirit's origin</note> from both. John 15:26.

<term>Divine Essence</term> (the fundamental nature of God), <term>Father</term>, <term>Son</term>, <term>Holy Spirit</term>, <term>Person</term> (an individual conscious being), <term>Comforter</term> (the Paraclete), <term>Understanding</term>, <term>Free Will</term>

<summary>This page explains the Christian doctrine of the Trinity, defining God as three distinct Persons—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—who share a single divine essence. It provides a formal theological definition of a "person" as a being with intellect and will.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Persons of God, Divine Essence, Father, Son, Holy Spirit, Theology, Catechism</keywords>

15. How then does this unity exist, since we are to believe in only One **God**?
   Father, Son, and Holy Spirit have one single, equal, inseparable essence and will, according to which they are not distinct but are One. John 10:30.

16. How do you mean that?
   In the three Persons, there is only One Eternity, Immensity <note>original: "Unmäßlichkeit," an archaic term meaning God's quality of being immeasurable and not confined by space</note>, Omnipresence, Wisdom, Omnipotence, Life, Goodness, Governance, and Blessedness <note>original: "Seligkeit," referring to the state of divine perfection and happiness</note>, in the highest perfection, and thus only One Deity.

17. Where does this triune **God** dwell?
   **God** dwells in heaven, in a light that no one can approach. Matthew 6:9.
->Psalm 115:3. 1 Timothy 6:16.<-

->revealed Himself?<-
   Yes, He has revealed Himself most lovely <note>original: "gar lieblich," suggesting a gentle or gracious unfolding of His nature</note> through His Word and works. 1 Samuel 3:21.

19. Through what kind of work has the Father especially revealed Himself?
   Through the work of Creation and the preservation of all things. Acts <note>original: "Ap. Gesch.," abbreviation for Apostelgeschichte (Acts of the Apostles)</note> 14:15, 17.

20. Through what else has the heavenly Father revealed Himself even more?
   Through the sending of His dear Son and the Holy Spirit into the world, so that we might come to Him. Galatians 4:4.
->Acts 2:4. Titus 3:6.<-

21. Through what kind of work has the
->Son revealed Himself?<-

## II. Of Creation, the Fall into Sin, and the Counsel of God for our Salvation.

### 1. Of Creation.

23. Where did the world and all things initially come from?
God the Father created in the beginning heaven and earth, and everything that is therein.
->Genesis 1:1. 1 Corinthians 8:6. Acts <note>original: "Ap. Gesch.," abbreviation for Apostelgeschichte (Acts of the Apostles)</note> 14:15.<-

24. Did the Son and the Holy Spirit not also work together in this?
Yes, for the heaven was made through the word of the Lord, and all its host through the Spirit of His mouth.
->Psalm 33:6. Ephesians 3:9.<-

25. From what did God create all things?
Out of nothing; <note>This refers to the theological concept of "creatio ex nihilo," creation out of nothing, rather than from pre-existing matter</note> solely by His almighty will all things have their being and are created.
->Psalm 148:5. Revelation 4:11.<-

26. How many kinds of creatures has God created?

<term>Creation</term> (the act of making the world); <term>Redemption</term> (saving humanity); <term>Holy Spirit</term> (the third person of the Trinity); <term>Sanctification</term> (the process of becoming holy); <term>Fall into Sin</term> (the biblical event of humanity's first sin); <term>Salvation</term> (eternal life/blessedness); <term>World</term> (the physical universe); <term>Will</term> (God's intent); <term>Creatures</term> (all created beings).

The angels, who are spirits and have no visible body. ->Hebrews 1:4, 7.<-

That they should serve Him and the faithful people on earth.

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Of Creation.</header>

35. **From what did God create the body of Adam?**
The Lord God made Adam's body from a clod of earth. Genesis 2:7. <note>original: "1 Mos.", abbreviation for "1. Moses," the German name for the book of Genesis</note>

36. **From where did he receive a rational, immortal soul?**
God breathed it into him. Genesis 2:7.

37. **What was the nature of the soul?**
It was a spirit that possessed intellect and will. 1 Corinthians 2:11.

38. **Why was man the most noble visible creature?**
Because he was created in the image of God, and according to His likeness. Genesis 1:26, chapter 5:1, James 3:9. <note>original: "Jac.", abbreviation for "Jacobus," the name for the Epistle of James</note>

39. **In what did this likeness consist?**
God gifted and adorned his intellect with the light of knowledge and wisdom; his will with love, holiness, and righteousness, according to the likeness of God. Colossians 3:10, Ephesians 4:24. See Questions 6 & 7. <note>original: "Fr.", abbreviation for "Frage" (Question)</note>

40. **What then was his soul?**
It was a bright mirror of the glory of God. 2 Corinthians 3:18, Romans 3:18.

41. **What was his body like?**
It was beautiful, enduring, and immortal. Wisdom of Solomon 2:23. <note>original: "Weish.", abbreviation for "Weisheit Salomos," a book of the Apocrypha/Deuterocanon</note>

42. **What further did God intend for him?**
He wished to have constant fellowship with him, to glorify His Son and image in him, and to dwell in him eternally. John 17:10, 2 Corinthians 6:16, 18.

<term>Rational soul</term> (the human soul, distinguished from animal life by its ability to reason and its eternal nature); <term>Image of God</term> (the theological concept that humans reflect God's character through wisdom, holiness, and righteousness); <term>Fellowship</term> (the intended intimate relationship between the Creator and humanity).

<summary>This page details the creation of Adam, explaining the dual nature of man as a physical body made from earth and a spiritual soul breathed by God. It defines the "Image of God" as a combination of intellect, wisdom, and moral purity.</summary>

<header>Of the Fall into Sin.</header>

43. **In what kind of state did God place Adam outwardly?**
He made him at once the regent and ruler over all creatures on earth.
->Genesis 1:28. <note>original: "1 Mos.", abbreviation for "1. Moses," the German name for the book of Genesis</note><-

44. **What kind of dwelling did God provide for him?**
God placed him in a very glorious garden of delights, which God himself had planted.
->Genesis 2:8.<-

45. **Did Adam also receive a pleasant companion?**
Yes, God took one of his ribs, and built a woman from it and brought her to him.
->Genesis 2:21, 22.<-

46. **How did man find himself in such circumstances?**
He was, both inwardly and outwardly, in a truly blessed and glorious state.

47. **How is this state otherwise called?**
It is commonly called the <term>State of Innocence</term> <note>The theological term for the period before humanity’s first sin</note>.

48. **Did man at that time know nothing of sin, death, and misery?**
No, he was without sin: death and all misery only came into the world through sin.
->Ecclesiastes 7:30 <note>original: "Pred.", abbreviation for "Der Prediger Salomo" (The Preacher Solomon)</note>, Romans 5:12.<-

## 2. Of the Fall into Sin.

49. **Did the first humans then not remain in this glorious state?**

<header>Of the Fall into Sin.</header>

<margin>Genesis 3:17. <note>original: "1 Mos.", abbreviation for "1. Moses," the German name for the book of Genesis</note></margin>
Alas, no; they unfortunately gambled it away <note>The German "verscherzet" implies losing something through carelessness, folly, or as if in a game</note> all too soon.

### 50. Through what did this happen?
<margin>Romans 5:15.</margin>
Through the sorrowful Fall into Sin.

### 51. Who then misled them into the Fall into Sin?
<margin>Genesis 3:1; Jude v. 6.</margin>
Satan, who along with many angels had already fallen away from GOD.

### 52. How did Satan bring this about?
<margin>Genesis 3:4, 5; 2 Corinthians 3:9.</margin>
Through his word of lies, he awakened in them a <term>proud desire</term> <note>The German "hochmüthiges Verlangen" refers to the sin of hubris—wanting to be greater than one's station</note> to become like GOD in omniscience and freedom.

### 53. What were humans led into by this pride?
<margin>Romans 5:19.</margin>
To disobedience against GOD and His prohibition.

### 54. What did that same prohibition say?
<margin>Genesis 2:17.</margin>
From the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, you shall not eat.

### 55. Why had GOD forbidden man the fruit of a certain tree?
To put his obedience to the test in such a minor matter, to see whether he would obey Him of his own free will.

### 56. Did man then become stubborn and not obey?
<margin>Genesis 3:6.</margin>
Upon the advice of Satan, and through the misuse of free will, man set aside the word of GOD and ate of the forbidden fruit.

# Of the Fall into Sin.

### 57. What followed upon this departure from GOD?
->The loss of the divine image.<-
<note>The "divine image" (Imago Dei) refers to the original state of perfection, holiness, and mirror-like reflection of God's character in which humans were created.</note>
->Romans 3:23.<-

### 58. What did man lose from his understanding through this?
->Spiritual wisdom and knowledge.<-
->Wisdom of Solomon 1:4. Psalm 25:14. Romans 1:22.<-

### 59. What did he lose from his will?
The love and holiness, indeed the entire life from GOD. Ephesians 2:1.

### 60. Did Satan gain entry to his inner self when man obeyed him?
Yes, indeed; since man accepted Satan’s word of lies, Satan has so permeated him with his <term>sin-poison</term> <note>The German "Sünden-Gift" views sin not just as a mistake, but as a literal toxin or venom that infects the human constitution</note> that he is corrupted to the core in body and soul, and has become similar and equal to his deceiver. Jeremiah 17:1.
->Ephesians 2:2. John 8:44.<-

### 61. In what way specifically has he become like him?
In hatred and enmity against GOD; he wanted nothing more to do with GOD.
->Romans 8:7. Genesis 3:8. <note>original: "1 Mos.", abbreviation for "1. Moses," the German name for the book of Genesis</note><-

### 62. How was his understanding damaged by this?
It became full of darkness, blindness, and folly, and entirely incapable of rightly recognizing GOD and divine things. Ephesians 4:18. Psalm 14:1.
->Jeremiah 5:21.<-

<meta>This page continues the numbered catechism from page 15, moving from the corruption of the human faculties to the specific punishments—the three types of death—resulting from the Fall.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
# Of the Fall into Sin.
<meta>catchword: blossom</meta>

### 63. How was his will corrupted?
He could no longer love GOD or trust Him; he became defiant toward everything good, and inclined toward everything evil. Titus 3:3. Jeremiah 6:10.

### 64. What punishment has he brought upon himself through his disobedience?
GOD’s curse and wrath, together with temporal, spiritual, and eternal death. Genesis 3:17, 19. <note>original: "1 Mos.", abbreviation for "1. Moses," the German name for the book of Genesis</note>

### 65. What is temporal death?
It is a bitter separation of the body and the soul. <note>This refers to physical death as we know it in time.</note>

### 66. What else is included in this?
All kinds of bodily misery: all illnesses, pains, plagues, and the judgments of GOD that occur on earth.

### 67. In what does spiritual death consist?
In a separation of the soul from the blessed communion with GOD, as well as in a lack of all spiritual strength and true comfort.
->Isaiah 59:2, 13. Ephesians 4:18. <note>original: "Jes." for "Jesaja" (Isaiah)</note><-

### 68. What else is included in this?
<term>Slavish fear</term>, <note>German: "knechtische Furcht"; the fear of a slave who expects only punishment, rather than the "filial fear" of a loving child</note> anxiety and the unrest of an evil conscience, and fleeing from GOD.
->Genesis 3:8, 10.<-

### 69. In what does eternal death consist?
In an eternal lack of all bliss, and in an eternal sensation of the utmost torment, amidst the greatest despair. Isaiah 66:24.
->Revelation 21:8. <note>original: "Offenb." for "Offenbarung" (Revelation)</note><-

### 70. Is there then nothing at all remaining unstained in the whole person?
Alas, no! There is neither in body nor soul, spi- <note>The text cuts off mid-word "Ge-", likely leading to "Gemüt" (disposition) or "Gedächtnis" (memory) on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page details the consequences of the Fall, defining "death" in three forms—physical, spiritual, and eternal—while emphasizing the total corruption of the human will and conscience. # Of the Fall of Man.
->3.<-
<meta>catchword: Of</meta>

### 83. **Is it also a sin if one omits something good?**

Yes, for whoever knows to do good and does it not, to him it is sin. James 4:17. <note>original: "Jac."</note>

### 84. **In how many ways can one commit <term>actual sin</term>?**
<note>Actual sin (German: "wirklich sündigen") refers to the specific acts, thoughts, or words an individual chooses to do, as opposed to the state of "original sin" one is born with.</note>

Either through ignorance, weakness, and hastiness, or through intent and malice.

### 85. **Can one make oneself a partaker of others' sins?**

Yes, through counsel, approval, command, failure to report or punish, and the like. Ezekiel 3:18. <note>original: "Ezech."</note>

### 86. **What do we deserve from God with our sins?**

God’s wrath and disfavor, along with <term>temporal</term> and eternal death.
<note>Temporal death refers to the physical death of the body in this life, whereas eternal death refers to spiritual separation from God.</note>

### 87. **Could no human then help himself out of this miserable wretchedness again?**

No, for what can a man give that he might ransom his soul again? Matthew 16:26. <note>original: "Matth."</note>

### 88. **Could no other creature help the fallen human back up?**

No, it costs too much to redeem their soul, and to make our cause right before the perfect justice and holiness of God. Psalm 49:8-9. <note>original: "Ps."</note>

### 89. **Could God then help the human again?**

With God no thing is impossible. Luke 1:37. <note>original: "Luc."</note>

<summary>This page explores the nuances of sin, including sins of omission and participating in the sins of others, while concluding that humanity is powerless to save itself without God's intervention.</summary>
<keywords>actual sin, omission, divine wrath, temporal death, eternal death, redemption, justice of God, salvation</keywords>

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>Of the Counsel of God.</header>
<meta>catchword: 95. How</meta>

### 3. Of the Counsel and Will of God for our Redemption.

90. **Did God not then destroy the world again for the sake of sinful humanity?**
No, but rather he bears, sustains, and governs it with great goodness, patience, and <term>longsuffering</term>.
<note>Longsuffering (German: *Langmuth*) is a traditional theological term for God's patient endurance and his choice to delay judgment to allow for repentance.</note>
Psalm 147:5; Psalm 36:7–8; Hebrews 1:3;
Matthew 5:45; Romans 2:4.

91. **Why does he do this?**
The faithful God takes no pleasure in the death of the ungodly; indeed, he wills that all people be helped, and that no one be lost.
<note>The phrase "be helped" (German: *geholfen werde*) in this context specifically refers to being saved or receiving salvation.</note>
Ezekiel 33:11; 1 Timothy 2:4; 2 Peter 3:9.

92. **How do we know this?**
From the gracious <term>decree</term> of God, which he formulated from eternity.
<note>A "decree" (German: *Rathschluß*) refers to a formal purpose or plan established by God's will before the creation of the world.</note>

93. **Did he then foresee our Fall from eternity?**
Yes, for nothing is hidden from his <term>omniscience</term>.
<note>Omniscience refers to God's attribute of having complete and infinite knowledge, including all future events.</note>
Hebrews 4:13; Isaiah 46:10; Wisdom of Solomon 8:8. <note>The "Wisdom of Solomon" is a book from the Apocrypha, which was often included in older German Bibles.</note>

94. **What now has he decided in his eternal decree?**
He has decided: 1) to have mercy upon the entire human race; 2) to redeem all people through his Son; 3) to call and sanctify them through his Spirit; and 4) to make the faithful eternally blessed.

<vocab>Counsel of God, Redemption, Omniscience, Decree, Patience, Longsuffering, Human race</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that God did not destroy humanity after the Fall but instead acted with patience, revealing an eternal plan to redeem all people through the work of the Trinity.</summary>
<keywords>Counsel of God, redemption, omniscience, divine decree, patience, salvation, mercy</keywords>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>Of the Counsel of God.</header>
<meta>catchword: 99. Which</meta>

95. **How is this purpose or <term>decree</term> of God regarding fallen humanity otherwise named?**
<note>The term "decree" (German: *Rathschluß*) refers to God's pre-ordained plan for the world.</note>
**The eternal Election of Grace.**
<note>Election of Grace (German: *Gnaden-Wahl*) is a theological term for God's decision to save humanity through His mercy rather than by human merit.</note>

96. **Why is it called the Election of Grace?**
Because God, out of pure love and grace, chose such a way or order through which lost humanity could once again become whole and <term>blessed</term>.
<note>The German *selig* encompasses being "blessed," "happy," and "saved" in an eternal sense.</note>

97. **But did God, in His Election of Grace, not initially choose only a portion of humanity to partake in such salvation, while rejecting the others?**
O no; for as has been said, God truly does not want anyone to be lost; He wills that all people be helped; therefore, the Son of God has redeemed them all, and the Holy Spirit calls them all. 2 Peter 3:9; 1 Timothy 2:4, 6; 1 John 2:2; Titus 2:11; Colossians 1:3.

98. **But Christ says that only few are chosen; where does that come from?**
Because all the others are unwilling to agree with God’s election and decree—that they should receive Christ in faith and truly turn to God—but instead prefer to run on the <term>broad way</term>; God foresaw this, and thus could not choose them for eternal life.
<note>The "broad way" is a reference to Matthew 7:13, symbolizing a life of sin or worldly ease that leads to destruction.</note>
->Matthew 23:37; chapter 22:3, 5.<-
->Acts 13:46.<-
<note>Acts 13:46 is abbreviated in the original as "Ap. Gesch." (Apostelgeschichte).</note>

<note>The following list translates the core vocabulary terms used on this page:</note>
<vocab>Election of Grace, Decree, God, Holy Spirit, Conversion, Christ, Election</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the "Election of Grace" as God's merciful plan to save all of humanity, clarifying that God does not predestine anyone to destruction, but rather people are lost because they reject His grace.</summary>
<keywords>election of grace, salvation for all, free will, divine decree, Holy Spirit, conversion, broad way</keywords>

### 99. Who then are the chosen?

They are all those whom God foresaw would accept His grace and calling, offered in Christ, believe in His Son, and persevere in such faith until the end.
<note>This reflects the doctrine of "Election in view of faith" (*Intuitu Fidei*), suggesting God's choice is based on His foreknowledge of a person's future belief.</note>
->Matthew 22:10, 14.<-
->Romans 8:29.<-

### 100. What did God look upon in this election?

Only upon Jesus Christ; that whoever would grasp Him in faith should be <term>blessed</term>, for He has chosen us in Christ through faith in the truth.
<note>The German *selig* is a rich term meaning both "blessed" and "eternally saved."</note>
->Ephesians 1:4. 2 Thessalonians 2:13.<-

### 101. What do you mean by that?

This <term>God-man</term> alone could be—and is appointed by God as—the only Mediator, the only Way, Cornerstone, Head, Savior, and Door through which we may be saved. God offers Him to us all, and wills that we should all receive Him and be saved; whoever resists this runs toward destruction.
<note>The "God-man" (*Gott-Mensch*) is a technical term for the union of divine and human natures in Jesus.</note>
->Acts 4:12.<-
->2 Corinthians 13:5.<-
<note>The original text cites 2 Cor. 13:15, likely a scribal error for 2 Cor. 13:5, which discusses testing one's faith.</note>

### 102. Did God also make this gracious will known to fallen humanity?

Yes, immediately in the first promise.
->Genesis 3:15.<-
<note>The original uses "1 Mose," the standard German shorthand for the First Book of Moses (Genesis).</note>

### 103. What did He promise in this first promise?

God promised a <term>Seed of the woman</term>, who would overcome Satan and [free] humanity from his

<meta>catchword: power</meta>

### 104. Was this promised 
->Redeemer then sent immediately?<-
No; God let four thousand years pass before He sent Him.
<note>This "four thousand years" reflects the traditional Ussher-style chronology common in the 17th and 18th centuries, which calculated the time from Creation to the birth of Christ based on biblical genealogies.</note>

### 105. What did God do in the meantime?
He allowed the promise to be explained ever more clearly, and prefigured the bloody death of the Redeemer through many sacrifices.
<note>The "sacrifices" refer to the Old Testament ritual laws which Christians traditionally interpret as "shadows" or "types" pointing toward the future sacrifice of Jesus.</note>

### 106. What more did He do?
Meanwhile, He renewed His law on Mount Sinai, which He had given into the heart of the first human. <margin>Exodus 19, 20; chapter 20.</margin>
<note>The original cites "2 Moses," the standard German way of referring to the Second Book of Moses, known in English as Exodus.</note>

### 107. What did God demand in this 
->Law?<-
That man should be of such a nature as He had initially created him according to His <term>image</term>, full of love for God and his neighbor.
<note>The "Image of God" (*Ebenbilde*) refers to the original state of moral perfection and harmony that humanity supposedly held before the Fall.</note>
<margin>Matthew 22:37–40.</margin>

### 108. What did He threaten against those who 
->would not fulfill these demands?<-
He spoke: Cursed be he who does not fulfill all the words of this law. <margin>Deuteronomy 27:26.</margin>
<note>The original cites "5 Moses," which is the Fifth Book of Moses, or Deuteronomy.</note>

### 109. What did He promise to those who 
->keep the law?<-
He spoke: The man who does these things shall live by them. <margin>Romans 10:5.</margin>

### 110. Could fallen man not keep 
->God's law perfectly?<-
No; for that, he had lost all strength through the <term>Fall</term>. <margin>Romans 8:3.</margin>
<note>The "Fall" (*Sündenfall*) refers to the first sin of Adam and Eve, which, in this theological tradition, resulted in a total loss of the human ability to satisfy God's moral requirements through their own effort.</note>

111. Could he <note>Referring to humanity's attempt to fulfill the Law mentioned on the previous page.</note> then not become righteous through the Law?

112. So must all people at that time <note>That is, the people living before the birth of Christ.</note> have been condemned?

<note>The term "blessed" (German: *selig*) carries the weight of being eternally saved and in a state of grace.</note>

113. Why then did the Law have to come before the appearance of the Redeemer?
So that through the Law, people might recognize their sin and helplessness <note>original: *Ohnmacht*</note>, and through its curse, be made all the more eager for the promised Physician and Savior.
<note>The metaphor of Christ as a "Physician" was a popular way to describe his role in healing the spiritual "sickness" of sin.</note>
->Romans 3:20; Galatians 3:24.<-

# III. Of Redemption.

## 1. Of the Redeemer’s Person and Offices.

114. When did the promised Redeemer finally come?
When the time was fulfilled, which God had determined for Himself, God sent His Son to redeem us. <margin>Galatians 4:4.</margin>

115. So God gave His own Son to be our Redeemer?
Yes, for God so loved the world that He gave His only-begotten Son. <margin>John 3:16.</margin>
->Romans 8:32.<-

Into the flesh <note>In this context, "flesh" refers to the assumption of human nature.</note>, so that God became man; and at the same time into the world. John 1:14.

He was conceived by the Holy Spirit and born of the Virgin Mary.

Yes, He said to His Father: I delight to do Your will, O my God. He gave Himself for all for redemption. Psalm 40:9.

He is called Jesus Christ.

A Rescuer, Savior, and <term>Bringer of Salvation</term>.
<note>original: *Seligmacher*. This specifically refers to one who grants eternal "blessedness" or salvation.</note>

### 121. Why is He called a Savior?
Because He was to acquire, proclaim, and impart true salvation.

### 122. What does the name Christ or Messiah mean?
One who is anointed with the Holy Spirit.
->Acts 10:38.<-

### 123. What kind of person is this Savior?
He is true God and true man in one person. Romans 9:5.

### 124. According to these two natures, is He a true Son of God?
Yes, He is entirely a Son of the most high Father,

### 125. But did He not take His flesh from Mary?

So that He could bestow an infinite validity and power upon His suffering and death, and thus reconcile God through His blood.

130. Does our Redeemer not have a **threefold office** in His <term>mediation</term> <note>The role of a mediator is to act as an intermediary or "go-between" to reconcile two parties—in this case, God and humanity.</note>?
Yes, He is a **High Priest**, **Prophet**, and **King**.

131. What has He done as a **High Priest** for our benefit?
He has reconciled us with God, and obtained true **salvation** through the sacrifice of His body, and through His **intercession** <note>Intercession refers to Christ continually praying or advocating on behalf of believers before God the Father.</note>. 
->Colossians 1:22. Hebrews 5:7.<-

132. What has He done as a **Prophet**?
He has proclaimed, and caused to be proclaimed to us, the salvation He obtained, along with the **order of salvation** <note>original: *Ordnung des Heils*. A central concept in Lutheran theology describing the step-by-step process by which a person receives the benefits of Christ, such as repentance, faith, and sanctification.</note>. 
->Ephesians 2:17. John 17:26.<-
->Acts 3:22, 23.<- <note>original: *Ap. Gesch.*, an abbreviation for *Apostelgeschichte* (Acts of the Apostles).</note>

133. What does He do as a **King** for our benefit?
He rules over His redeemed in His **kingdom of grace**, drives back their enemies, and makes them partakers of the obtained salvation.
->1 Corinthians 15:25. Psalm 89:18, 19.<-

134. Has our Redeemer always been in the **same state**?
No; first He was in the **state of humiliation**, and now He is in the **state of exaltation** <note>These two "states" (*Stände*) refer to the different modes of Christ's existence: His earthly life of suffering and service (humiliation) and His current reign in glory (exaltation).</note>.
->Hebrews 1:3.<-

In him there is no sin: But was he tempted in all things, just as we are, yet without sin? <note>While Christ shared human physical limitations, the text emphasizes his moral perfection. The question mark here reflects the rhetorical style of the original German.</note>
->1 John 3:5. Hebrews 4:15. chapter 2:18.<-

137. What did he **set aside** during this <term>humiliation</term> <note>original: *Erniedrigung*. In theology, this refers to the "State of Humiliation," the period from Christ's conception to his burial where he voluntarily refrained from fully using his divine power.</note>?
The full and constant use of his divine glory, which he divested himself of for a time. Philippians 2:6, 7.

138. Why did he have to **humble** himself so deeply?
So that he might atone for our **pride**, and rescue us from our **misery** <note>original: *Unseligkeit*. This refers to a state of being "un-blessed" or spiritual wretchedness resulting from the fall of man.</note>.

139. What did our **misery** consist of, from which he wanted to rescue us?
In the **guilt of sin** and the **punishment of sin**.

140. Has he then freed us from both of these evils?
Yes, he has removed both guilt and punishment.

141. How has he freed us from the **guilt of sin**?
By allowing the same to be reckoned to him, and paying for it as our <term>guarantor</term> <note>original: *Bürge*. A legal term for someone who takes responsibility for another person’s debt or behavior; a "surety."</note>: For the LORD cast the sins of us all upon him. Psalm 69:5. Isaiah 53:6.

142. With what did he **pay** for it?
With the **ransom** of his precious blood and life, which he, under a perfect
->**Obedience**<- <note>The text ends here, suggesting the sentence continues on the next page regarding Christ's "active obedience" to God's law.</note>

Death is the wages of sin. Romans 6:23.

Yes, by God’s grace he tasted death for everyone. Hebrews 2:9.

Yes, when he cried out: My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?
Matthew 27:46. <note>In Lutheran theology, Christ's cry of dereliction on the cross is understood as him experiencing the actual spiritual suffering of hell and separation from God on behalf of humanity.</note>

Yes, he did not come to abolish the law, but to fulfill it. Matthew 5:17.

Referring to Colossians 2:14, this signifies a handwritten bond or "certificate of debt" that documented humanity's failure to keep God's law.</note> that was against us, and made us free. Colossians 2:14. John 8:32, 36.
Galatians 5:1. chapter 3:13.

# Of Christ the Redeemer.

### 2.) Of the State of Christ’s Exaltation.

**156. But did our Redeemer remain in death?**
No, **God** has raised **Jesus** from the dead, and given him glory, so that we might have faith and hope in **God**.
1 Peter 1:21.

**157. How is our faith and hope properly grounded in the resurrection of Christ?**
If we believe that **Jesus** was given over to death for the sake of our sins, then his resurrection assures us of our righteousness before **God**, because **God** has loosed the bonds of death for our <term>Surety</term> <note>original: *Bürgen*. A legal and theological term for someone who takes on the debt of another and guarantees payment.</note>. Romans 4:25. Acts 2:24.

**158. What did the Lord Jesus lay aside in his resurrection?**
He laid aside the assumed <term>form of a servant</term> <note>original: *Knechtsgestalt*. This refers to the humble, mortal condition Christ took on during his earthly life, as described in Philippians 2:7.</note> and all the weaknesses of human nature: but not human nature itself.

**159. What, by contrast, did he take up?**
The full use of his divine glory; that he now lives, he lives to **God**; yes, this **God-Man** is now the true **God** and eternal life. John 17:5. Romans 6:10. 1 John 5:20. John 20:28.

**160. Who has thus exalted our Mediator?**
**God** his Father has thus exalted him, as a testimony of his pleasure in his <term>satisfaction</term> <note>original: *Genugthuung*. The doctrine that Christ's work "satisfied" or paid the debt of justice owed to God for human sin.</note>.
Philippians 2:9.

**161. How has he exalted him and led him into glory?**
1.) Through the resurrection from the dead. 2.) Through the ascension, by which he was taken up into glory. 3.) Through the seating at his right hand. 1 Peter 1:21. 1 Timothy 3:16.
->Psalm 110:1.<-

**162. Why has he led him into glory?**
So that he might place him in the position to impart the acquired salvation to us; and to protect us in the possession of the same against our enemies. Acts 5:31. Romans 14:9. Psalm 45:5.
->Psalm 110:2.<-

**163. Is human nature in Christ established in the same full glory?**
Certainly, for it was the Father's good pleasure that in this firstborn from the dead all fullness, yes, the whole <term>fullness of the Godhead</term> <note>original: *Fülle der Gottheit*. A theological concept from Colossians referring to the totality of divine nature and powers dwelling in Christ's physical body.</note> should dwell bodily. Colossians 1:18, 19.
->Colossians 2:9. Matthew 28:18.<-

**164. What is the primary business of the exalted Savior?**
His intercession for us before the Father.
->Hebrews 7:25. 1 John 2:1.<-

**165. Prove this to me!**
Christ is at the right hand of **God** and intercedes for us. Romans 8:34. Hebrews 9:24. Hebrews 7:25.

**166. For whom does he pray?**
For all people, just as he gave himself as a <term>redemption</term> <note>original: *Erlösung*. The act of buying back or liberating someone from debt or captivity; here, the spiritual deliverance from sin.</note> for all; yet especially for his believers. 1 Timothy 2:6. Isaiah 53:12. John 17:20.

<page-num>33</page-num>

# Of Christ the Redeemer.

**167. What does he ask on behalf of the unbelievers?**
Opportunity and grace for <term>repentance</term> <note>original: *Buße*. In this theological context, it refers to a fundamental turning away from sin and toward God, often prompted by divine grace.</note>, and the forgiveness of sins. Luke 13:8; 23:34.

**168. What does he ask on behalf of the believers?**
All kinds of divine powers of grace, for growth in goodness, and for perseverance until the end. John 17:9, 15; Luke 22:32.

**169. How does his intercession take place?**
He presents his merit to the heavenly Father and desires that, for the sake of that merit, the acquired <term>benefits of salvation</term> <note>original: *Heils-Güter*. These are the spiritual blessings won by Christ’s sacrifice, including peace, righteousness, and eternal life.</note> be shared with mankind. John 17:24.

**170. What else has he undertaken following his exaltation?**
The establishment and rule of his <term>kingdom of grace</term> <note>original: *Gnaden-Reich*. A term describing Christ's spiritual rule over the hearts of people through the Gospel, distinct from his "kingdom of power" over the universe.</note> on earth.

**171. What kind of kingdom has he destroyed through his death?**
The kingdom of Satan. 1 John 3:8.

**172. What kind of kingdom has he established in its place?**
The kingdom of **God**, over which the Father has set him as ruler. Psalm 2:6; Luke 11:20.

**173. Through whom has he established it?**
Through his Holy Spirit, in the ministry of his Apostles, whom he equipped for that purpose. Acts 1:5, 8.

**174. Which is his kingdom?**
It is not a kingdom of this world, but it is the holy Christian Church. John 18:36; Ephesians 1:22.

**175. Does he himself also exercise dominion in this kingdom?**

**176. Who are the people of his kingdom?**
They are the believers from all nations. They are his <term>special people</term> <note>original: *Volk des Eigenthums*. A biblical phrase from Titus 2:14 referring to a people claimed by God as his own particular possession.</note>, who are eager for good works. Titus 2:14.

**177. What do they enjoy under his rule?**
Righteousness, peace, and joy in the Holy Spirit. Romans 14:17.

**178. How does he rule this kingdom of his?**
Not in a worldly way, but in a spiritual manner, through the Word, which is the <term>scepter of his kingdom</term> <note>This metaphor illustrates that Christ does not rule by force or physical weapons, but by the power of the Gospel message.</note>. Psalm 110:2.

Yes, he is a **Lord** of all lords, and a King of all kings; indeed, all creatures are subject to him. Revelation 17:14; 1 Timothy 6:15; Psalm 8:7 <note>original: *seq.* (Latin *sequentes*), meaning "and the following verses."</note>.

181. **Who sends the Holy Spirit?**
The Lord Jesus sent him from the Father after his <term>exaltation</term> <note>original: *Erhöhung*. This refers to Christ’s return to glory through his resurrection and ascension into heaven.</note>, as he promised: "If I go away, I will send him to you." John 16:7.

182. **Upon whom did the Lord Jesus first pour out his Holy Spirit?**
Upon his disciples and apostles, who were <term>clothed with power from on high</term> <note>A biblical expression (Luke 24:49) meaning to be equipped with divine strength and authority.</note> at the first Festival of Pentecost. Acts 2:4; Luke 24:49.

->**the apostles?**<-
He <term>convicted</term> <note>original: *bestrafte*. While this can mean "punished," in the context of John 16:8 it refers to the Spirit exposing the world's sin and convincing people of their need for God.</note> the world because of its unbelief. John 16:8.

184. ->**What else did he do?**<-
He transfigured or glorified the Lord Jesus through the teaching and miracles of the apostles. John 16:14; Acts 4:10.

185. ->**What, then, did the apostles preach?**<-
They preached, according to Christ's command, the Gospel to <term>every creature</term> <note>original: *aller Creatur*. In older English and German, this encompasses all of humanity and the whole of creation.</note>. Mark 16:16.

186. ->**What is such a Gospel?**<-
It is the comforting message that God is reconciled with the world through Christ; and that he wishes to save all those who believe in Christ.

->**preaching of the Gospel?**<-
That people should convert and believe in Christ. Acts 26:18; ch. 16:31.

188. ->**Does this Gospel then concern all**<-
->**people?**<-
Yes, for God wants all to come to the knowledge of the truth. 1 Timothy 2:4; Colossians 1:23.

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Holy Spirit from page 33, shifting from the historical sending of the Spirit at Pentecost to the internal, spiritual "office" or work the Spirit performs within the individual believer.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>Of the Office of the Holy Spirit.</header>

189. **Does he also want to give his Holy Spirit to everyone?**
Yes, the Father in heaven wants to give the Holy Spirit to all who ask him for it.
->Luke 11:13.<-

190. **What is the office of the Holy Spirit once he is given to a person?**
The office of the Holy Spirit is to impart or apply to mankind—within the <term>Order of Salvation</term> <note>original: *Heils-Ordnung*. A theological framework describing the series of steps (such as repentance, faith, and sanctification) through which a person receives the benefits of Christ.</note>—that salvation intended by the Father, acquired by the Son, and proclaimed through his power.

191. **How does the Holy Spirit go about performing his duties?**
He makes use of an office of teaching and reproving, as well as an office of discipline and comfort. <note>The "office of reproving" (*Straf-Amt*) refers to the Spirit's work in convicting the conscience of sin, while "discipline" (*Zucht*) refers to the ongoing spiritual training or nurturing of a believer's character.</note>

192. **What does the Holy Spirit intend to accomplish in people through this?**
Through teaching and reproving, he intends to introduce them into the Order of Salvation. Through guiding or disciplining and comforting, he intends to preserve and strengthen them within it.

193. **What benefit does one gain by giving space and a hearing to such workings of the Holy Spirit?**
One is converted and becomes a believer, is justified, reborn, and purified, and is prepared and made fit for eternal life. 2 Cor. 6:11. <note>This citation likely refers to 1 Corinthians 6:11, which lists being washed, sanctified, and justified in the name of the Lord Jesus and by the Spirit of God.</note>

194. **What else is the office of the Holy Spirit called?**
Sanctification. Romans 15:16.

195. **How necessary is sanctification?** <note>The German *Heiligung* refers to the process of being made holy or consecrated to God.</note>
Without sanctification no one will see the Lord.

196. **What was the counsel of God regarding such sanctification?**
That through it we should be renewed again into the image of God, for which we were created and which we have lost.

197. **What then is the Order of Salvation, into which the Holy Spirit intends to introduce us through the Gospel?** <note>The <term>Order of Salvation</term> (*Heils-Ordnung*) is the theological sequence of steps—such as repentance and faith—by which a person receives God's grace.</note>
It is repentance toward God and faith in our Lord *Jesus*.

198. **To what end should we apply this Order of Salvation?**
Through repentance we should lay aside sinful evil; through faith we should receive and put on the restorative good from Christ. Through repentance we turn away from sin; through faith we turn toward God.

199. **Now give me a brief summary of that which we have learned so far, and what we intend to learn hereafter!** <note>original: *Summa*</note>
Thus far we have learned what the Triune God has done for us; now we shall also learn what we must do so that we may attain salvation and fellowship with God.

## 2. Of Repentance and Faith.

### 1.) Of Repentance.

200. **What is repentance?** <note>The German word *Busse* carries the sense of both "penitence" (feeling sorry) and "repentance" (an active turning away from sin).</note>
It is an internal change of the mind and the will.

201. **How was man minded before his repentance?**
<margin>Romans 8:7.</margin>
His fleshly mind was an enmity against God. <note>In this context, "fleshly" refers to worldly desires and impulses that lead away from spiritual life.</note>

202. **Does he remain an enemy of God after his repentance?**
No, he becomes a friend and a lover of God.

203. **How was man previously minded toward sin?**
He loved sin and sought all opportunities to sin.

204. **Is he still minded in this way after his repentance?**
<margin>Psalm 97:10.</margin>
No, he hates sin and flees all opportunities to sin.

205. **What does this internal change of the mind bring about?**
<margin>Romans 12:2; Ephesians 4:17, 25 etc.</margin> <note>original: *seq.* (Latin abbreviation for *sequens*, meaning "and following")</note>
An external change of the entire conduct, in gestures, words, and works. <note>The <term>conduct</term> (*Wandel*) refers to one's manner of living or daily walk in the world.</note>

206. **From what does one convert in true repentance?**
<margin>Acts 26:18.</margin>
From sin and the power of Satan.

<vocab>Repentance, Faith, Change of Mind, Enmity, God, Sin, Conduct, Satan, Conversion</vocab>

<summary>This page defines repentance as an internal transformation of the mind and will that results in a visible change in behavior. It describes the shift from being an enemy of God who loves sin to a friend of God who actively avoids it.</summary>
<keywords>repentance, conversion, enmity with God, spiritual transformation, conduct, sin, Satan, mind and will</keywords>

->Of Repentance.<-

207. **To whom does one turn?**
To God and His fellowship.

208. **If one is to turn away from sin, what is required for that?**
That one must learn to recognize his <term>inherited and actual sin</term> with shame and sorrow. <margin>Psalm 51:5.</margin> <note>Inherited sin refers to "Original Sin" passed down through humanity, while actual sin refers to the specific wrongdoings an individual commits during their life.</note>

209. **Is it enough if one recognizes his sin?**
No, one must also sincerely repent of it, hate it, and loathe it. <margin>2 Corinthians 7:10, 11.</margin>

210. **Through what is this hatred brought about?**
Through the knowledge and feeling of the wrath of God, especially as it has been revealed in the suffering of Christ. <margin>Psalm 32:3, 4.</margin>

211. **What does the hatred of sin bring with it?**
A sincere intention to cast off all sins and to avoid them in the future. <margin>Proverbs 28:13.</margin> <note>original: "Spr. Sal." referring to the Proverbs of Solomon.</note>

212. **What might be the best motivating cause for true repentance?**
The heartfelt love of the Father and Son, shown to us lost sinners in the bitter suffering and death of Christ, whereby we may also at the same time best see the damnable loathsomeness of sin. <margin>1 John 4:9, 10.</margin>

213. **Can we begin true conversion out of our own strength?**
Ah no, but the <term>prevenient grace</term> of the calling God, re-acquired through Christ, is always ready to lead us into it, if we do not hinder it. <margin>Titus 2:11, 12.</margin> <note>Prevenient grace (*vorkommende Gnade*) is a theological term for the grace of God that "comes before" any human action, enabling a person to respond to God's call despite their fallen state.</note>

It concludes by stating that human effort alone cannot initiate conversion; rather, it is made possible through God's prevenient grace.</summary>

# Of Faith,

214. **How does grace act when it touches the sinner for conversion?**

Through its beam of light, it often penetrates his intellect and conscience, so that he sees he is lacking something important, and at the same time stirs a longing or secret sighing in his will for something better.

215. **How might this develop into conversion?**

If the sinner cherishes these first sparks and brings them into prayer, then grace will continue its work within him.

216. **But through what does one turn to God?**

Through faith in Jesus Christ: No one comes to the Father except through him.

->John 14:6; Acts 26:18.<- <note>original: "Ap. Gesch." refers to *Apostelgeschichte*, the Acts of the Apostles.</note>

217. **Of what main parts does true Christian repentance consist?**

It consists of two main parts: namely, in <term>remorse</term> and in faith. <note>In this context, "remorse" (Reue) implies a deep spiritual sorrow for sin, rather than just regret.</note>

218. **What use is repentance, and what does one obtain through it?**

The forgiveness of sins and a merciful God. <margin>Isaiah 1:16, 18; Hebrews 8:12.</margin>

It is a divine light through which the repentant soul recognizes Jesus Christ as its only Savior with <term>approval</term>; and a living divine <note>The word "approval" (Beyfall) here refers to the soul’s internal "assent" or "acceptance" of Christ’s role as Savior. # Of Faith.

power, through which it <note>The "it" refers back to the "repentant soul" mentioned on the previous page.</note> seizes and appropriates Christ and His benefits with heartfelt confidence.

### 220. How many parts, then, does true faith have?

Three: **Knowledge**, **Assent**, and **Confidence**. Or otherwise: Knowledge with assent; desire with sighing; seizing with peace. <note>In Lutheran theology, faith was often divided into these three steps: *notitia* (knowledge of the facts), *assensus* (agreement that they are true), and *fiducia* (personal trust or confidence).</note>

### 221. Where does faith come from?

It is a work and gift of **God** the Holy Spirit in the soul, through the Word of the living **God**.
->Colossians 2:12; Ephesians 1:19.<-
->Romans 10:17.<-

### 222. What is the sole object of faith?

The constant <term>object</term> of faith is the triune **God**; but most especially the Mediator **Jesus Christ**. <note>original: "Vorwurf". In 18th-century German, this term meant the "object" or "subject matter" one focuses on, rather than a "reproach" or "accusation" as it does in modern German.</note>
->Deuteronomy 5:7; 1 Timothy 2:5.<- <note>original: "5 Mos. 5, 7" refers to the Fifth Book of Moses, which is Deuteronomy.</note>

### 223. By what is true faith recognized?

It is recognized by its power and by its fruits.

### 224. What kind of power does faith have?

It has a justifying power, through which we obtain forgiveness of sins for Christ’s sake; and a sanctifying power, through which we are freed from the dominion of sin and overcome the world.
->Romans 4:5.<-
->Acts 15:9; 1 John 5:4.<- <note>original: "Ap. Gesch." refers to *Apostelgeschichte*, the Acts of the Apostles.</note>

# Of Faith.

fruits of the Spirit, or good works, such as love, obedience, trust in **God**, and the like.
->2 Peter 1:5–7; Galatians 5:22.<-

### 226. Is faith active through love?

Yes, and thereby it proves itself to be a true faith, valid before **God**. Galatians 5:6.

### 227. Why are such good works called fruits of the Spirit or of faith?

Because the Holy Spirit in faith is, as it were, the sap that drives these fruits forth in the "trees of righteousness." Isaiah 61:3. <note>This botanical metaphor was common in 18th-century pietism, suggesting that good works are not forced, but grow naturally from a soul "planted" in God's grace.</note>

### 228. Is there not also a false faith?

Yes; many people have a dead, hypocritical, and sham faith. <note>original: "Todten-Heuchel- und Schein-Glauben". This refers to an intellectual belief that does not change a person's behavior or heart.</note>

### 229. Where does false faith come from?

It is taken by human powers from the mere letter of the Scripture; it is therefore a merely human imagination. <note>The author distinguishes here between a "living" faith granted by the Spirit and a "dead" faith created simply by reading words without spiritual experience.</note>

### 230. What does this faith lack?

It lacks both the aforementioned divine light and divine power, and consequently also the good fruits. James 2:17, 26.

### 231. What benefit does one have if one enters into the <term>order of repentance</term> and faith?

One is freed in this order from all misery and becomes a partaker of all salvation acquired through Christ. <note><term>order of repentance</term> (original: "Ordnung der Busse") refers to the systematic process of spiritual conversion—recognizing sin, feeling sorrow, and turning toward God.</note>

# Of Justification.

### 232. In what does the misery consist from which one is thus freed?
In the guilt of sin, in the dominion of sin, and in the punishment of sin. <note>The author uses "unseligkeit" (literally "un-blessedness") to describe the state of the soul before grace—a condition of spiritual wretchedness.</note>

### 233. Is a believer thus freed from all these evils?
Yes; the guilt of sin is forgiven him, the tyrannical dominion of sin is broken, and the temporal and eternal punishment is remitted. ->2 Samuel 12:13; Romans 6:14; Zephaniah 3:15.<-

### 234. In what does the salvation consist that the penitent obtain through faith?
They become children of **God** through faith. They are united with Christ and become members of his body. They become temples of the Holy Spirit, who dwells and works within them. ->Galatians 3:26; Ephesians 5:30; 1 Corinthians 3:16.<- <note>Penitent (original: "Bußfertige") refers to those who have undergone a sincere change of heart and turned away from sin.</note>

### 3. Of <term>Justification</term> and Rebirth.
<note><term>Justification</term> (original: "Rechtfertigung") is the legal metaphor for God declaring a sinner righteous, while <term>Rebirth</term> (original: "Wiedergeburt") refers to the inner transformation of the person's nature.</note>

### 235. What must we note regarding the order of these matters?
That both of these, justification and rebirth, belong together and happen at the same time, so that one cannot exist without the other.

### 236. Why can one not exist without the other?
If someone is to be justified, they must surely live—namely, the life of faith and of the... <note>The text breaks here; the next page continues the thought regarding the "fruits of the Spirit."</note>

# Of Justification.

237. In what way must one distinguish between
->justification and rebirth?<-

Justification actually happens with **God**: Rebirth happens with and in us. Justification deals with the guilt and punishment of sin: Rebirth is set against the indwelling evil of sin or spiritual death. <note>In Lutheran theology, "Justification" is often described as a "forensic" or legal act where God declares a person righteous from the outside, whereas "Rebirth" is the internal transformation of the person's character.</note>

### 1.) Of Justification.

238. What kind of transaction is the justification
->of faith?<-

It is the gracious transaction of **God**, in which the righteousness of **Jesus Christ** is credited to the penitent sinner by **God**, and through which his sins are forgiven. Romans 3:24;
->chapter 4:6, 7, 8.<- <note>The term "credited" (original: *zugerechnet*) is a central concept in the Reformation, meaning that Christ's perfection is put onto the sinner's account like a debt being paid by someone else.</note>

239. What is understood here by the righteousness
->of Jesus Christ?<-

His entire obedience, which he performed in our place for the Heavenly Father, both in fulfilling his law and in bearing our punishments. Romans 5:19; Matthew 5:17;
->Isaiah 53:5.<-

->righteousness?<-
