# Of Justification.

...the right to the forfeited <term>salvation</term> has been acquired again. <note>original: "Seligkeit." This refers to the state of eternal happiness and union with God which was lost at the Fall of Man.</note> 2 Corinthians 5:21.

### 241. How can Christ’s obedience be
### credited to sinners?

Because Christ took their place in the divine court and allowed our sins to be <term>credited</term> to Him: thus Christ's righteousness and obedience are credited to them, as if they had performed it themselves. <note>In theology, this is called "imputation," where one person’s record is legally applied to another’s account.</note> Isaiah 53:6, 11, 12; Romans 4:24, 25.

### 242. Why is this righteousness
### called a "righteousness of faith"?

Because faith in Christ (which takes hold of Christ entirely) primarily appropriates this righteousness for itself. Romans 3:22; chapter 4:5; Philippians 3:9.

### 243. What do we obtain from God for the
### sake of the credited righteousness of Christ,
### when it is seized in faith?

The forgiveness of sins and peace with **God** in our conscience. <note>The author emphasizes that "peace" is not just an abstract idea, but something felt internally in the conscience once the "legal debt" of sin is removed.</note> Ephesians 1:7; Romans 5:1, 2.

### 244. Which sins does God forgive?

Each and every one that is acknowledged and repented for with a <term>penitent</term> heart. <note>Penitence involves a sincere recognition of wrongdoing and a desire to change.</note> Psalms 103:3; 1 John 1:7; Psalms 51:5.

### 245. In what does the forgiveness
### of sins consist?

In a gracious remission of the debt and the punishment for the sake of Christ, who paid the debt and bore the punishment. Psalms 32:5; Micah 7:18, 19; 1 John 1:9.

### 246. So does justification actually 
### become the person’s own?

Yes,

# Of Justification.

Yes, it is powerfully felt in the conscience and sealed in the heart through the testimony of the Holy Spirit. Romans 8:16; Ephesians 4:30. <note>In this context, "sealing" refers to a divine guarantee or internal certainty given to the believer.</note>

### 247. What is the further fruit of justification 
### ->in the believing heart?<-

That justifying faith also proves its <term>sanctifying</term> power by resisting all sins, cleansing itself from them, and continuing with sanctification in the fear of God. <note>Sanctification (Heiligung) is the process of growing in holiness and moral character after being "justified" or declared righteous by God.</note>
Romans 5:21; chapter 6:14, 18, 22; 2 Corinthians 7:1.

### 248. How should I understand it when it is said: 
### ->Faith justifies?<-

It is because faith takes hold of the rich grace of God which stands open in <term>Christ's merit</term>, and specifically appropriates it for itself. Faith is like our hand in this matter. <note>The author uses the "hand" metaphor to explain that faith is merely the instrument that receives a gift; the hand itself does not earn the gift.</note> Romans 1:17; chapter 3:28.

### 249. So it is also said: 
### ->Christ’s merit justifies?<-

Quite right, for Christ’s merit is the <term>effective cause</term>; grace offers it to us, but faith takes hold of it. <note>The "effective cause" is the actual work or payment that makes the result possible.</note> Romans 5:19.

### 250. Is it not also said: 
### ->God or the grace of God justifies?<-

Yes, for God with His grace is the <term>original cause</term>; God's grace credits the merit of Christ to faith. <note>The "original cause" refers to the source or motivation—God's love and mercy.</note>
Romans 3:24–26; chapter 8:33; Philippians 3:9.

### 251. Thus all three—the grace of God, the 
### merit of Christ, and our faith—come 
### together in our justification 
### ->in the divine court?<-

## 2.) Of the New Birth.

252. **What is the new birth?**
It is the gracious act of God, in which a person is raised from spiritual death and placed into spiritual life; and being changed in heart and mind, is made a <term>partaker of the divine nature</term> and character. <note>"Partaker of the divine nature" (from 2 Peter 1:4) does not mean becoming a god, but rather sharing in God's moral qualities, like holiness and love.</note> <margin>1 John 3:14; John 5:25; Ephesians 4:23; 2 Peter 1:4.</margin>

253. **How necessary is the new birth?**
Unless someone is born again, they cannot see the kingdom of God.
->John 3:3.<-

254. **Why is it called a birth?**
Because something is brought forth in the person that was not there before. <note>The author emphasizes that this is a creative act of God, not just a minor self-improvement.</note> <margin>Galatians 4:19.</margin>
->1 Corinthians 2:16.<-

255. **What is brought forth there?**
The image and mind of Christ, a new person, a new spiritual life, and a powerful drive toward everything good. <margin>Ephesians 4:23, 24.</margin>

256. **What is spiritual death?**
It is such a state where one is incapable of and dead to everything spiritually good, but on the other hand to everything

### 265. What is the new man's nourishment for growth?

Christ is the new man's milk and strong food in faith. John 6:35.

### 266. What further takes place during this growth of the new man?

A constant purification from all clinging sins. Hebrews 12:1. 2 Corinthians 7:1.

### 267. Do sins still cling to those who are reborn?

Yes, the root of <term>original sin</term> <note>The original German "Erbsünde" refers to the theological concept of an inherited sinful nature.</note> still sticks in them, even though it has been stripped of its dominion. Romans 7:18.

### 268. Are they also sometimes overtaken by original sin?

Yes, they are sometimes overtaken in thoughts, words, and deeds. Disordered inclinations toward anger, laziness, pride, self-interest, stubbornness, impatience, <term>lust</term> <note>The original term "Geilheit" in this historical religious context refers to carnal desires or worldly wantonness.</note>, etc., stir within.

### 269. How do they purify themselves from such clinging bad habits?

Through daily, faithful appropriation of the blood of Jesus Christ, and through a constant struggle against the indwelling sin. 1 John 1:7.
->Hebrews 12:1. 2 Corinthians 7:1.<-

->The wicked (says the Lord) have no peace. Isaiah 48:22; 57:20, 21.<- <note>original: "Ef." for *Esaias* (Isaiah).</note>

302. **What is the third benefit that flows from the fellowship of Christ?**
Spiritual freedom.

303. **Are believers free?**
Because the Son of God makes them free, they are truly free. John 8:36.

304. **From what are they free?**
They are released from the law that held them captive. Romans 7:6.

305. **From which law?**
Primarily from the Jewish ecclesiastical and civil law. <note>The original term *Policey-Gesetz* refers to the general administrative and social regulations of the community rather than modern "police" work.</note>

306. **How long was this law to last?**
Until Christ, who broke down the fence, namely the law, which was established in commandments. Ephesians 2:14.

307. **Are believers also free from the disciplinary and moral law that is contained in the Ten Commandments?** <note>The text uses *Zucht- und Sitten-Gesetz* to distinguish between outward discipline and inward morality.</note>
They are free from the curse and compulsion of the law, because they perform the duties required by the law voluntarily. Meanwhile, it nonetheless remains a lamp for them. Galatians 3:13, Romans 6:14, 1 Timothy 1:8, 9, Psalm 119:105.

308. **From what else are believers free?**
They are free from the dominion of sin, and released from an evil conscience. Romans 6:14, Hebrews 10:22.

<meta>This page continues the numbered sequence of questions regarding the "Blessings of Salvation," specifically focusing on "Spiritual Freedom" which was introduced at the end of page 55. It transitions from freedom from the Law to freedom from the consequences and power of sin.</meta>

<header>Of the Blessings of Salvation.</header>

### 309. How does the liberation from the dominion of sin occur?
In that they have surrendered themselves to the dominion of grace, through righteousness unto eternal life, through Jesus Christ our Lord.
->Romans 5:21; chapter 6:16.<-

### 310. How does the liberation from an evil conscience occur?
Through justification, whereby guilt and punishment are remitted; and through sanctification, whereby the conscience is enlightened and governed by the Holy Spirit. Romans 5:1; 2 Corinthians 3:17.
->Romans 8:16.<-

### 311. Are they then also at the same time liberated from servile fear?
Yes; the servile fear <note>original: *knechtische Furcht*. In Lutheran theology, this refers to the fear a slave has for a master—obeying only to avoid punishment—which is replaced in the believer by "filial fear," the loving reverence a child has for a parent.</note> of God and His temporal and eternal punishments ceases along with the evil conscience: For perfect love casts out fear. 2 Timothy 1:7; Romans 8:15.
->1 John 4:18.<-

### 312. Are they also free from the wrath of God?
Yes; they are preserved from wrath through Christ, after they have been made righteous through His blood. Romans 5:9; Isaiah 54:9.

### 313. Are they also free from death?
Yes, from temporal, spiritual, and eternal death. John 8:51.

### 314. In what way are they free from temporal death?
In that they do not have to endure it as a punishment. <note>While believers still experience physical ("temporal") death, it is viewed here not as a legal penalty for sin, but as a transition to eternal life.</note>

<meta>vocabulary: liberation, sin, grace, righteousness, justification, sanctification, Holy Spirit, servile fear, wrath of God, death</meta>

<meta>This page continues the numbered sequence from the previous page regarding the "Blessings of Salvation." It specifically addresses how the believer’s "spiritual freedom" applies to physical death, spiritual death, and the influence of the devil.</meta>

<header>Of the Blessings of Salvation.</header>

315. **Must believers not die just as well as unbelievers?**
They must indeed die, but death cannot hold them, and they shall live again, even though they die: Death is swallowed up in victory, for sin, as the sting of death, is forgiven them.
->John 11:25. Isaiah 26:19.<-
->1 Corinthians 15:55, 56.<-

316. **How is the death of believers to be regarded?**
As a gentle sleep, and a passing to the Father. In faith they fear it as little as a weary person fears their bed of rest.
->Daniel 12:13. Isaiah 57:2. John 11:11.<-
->1 Corinthians 15:20.<-

317. **How have they become free from spiritual death?**
In rebirth <note>original: *Wiedergeburt*. In Lutheran theology, this refers to the process of being spiritually "born again" through faith and baptism.</note> they have attained spiritual life through faith in Jesus' name.
->John 20:31. Question 252.<-

318. **Are they also free from eternal death, hell, and damnation?**
No harm shall come to them from the second death <note>A biblical concept from the Book of Revelation referring to the final spiritual judgment.</note>, for Jesus has delivered them from the wrath to come.
->1 Thessalonians 1:10. Revelation 2:11.<-

319. **But are they also free from the devil?**
Yes, they are rescued from the dominion <term>dominion: *Oberkeit*, meaning the ruling authority or sovereignty</term> of darkness: He can no longer master their understanding or will, nor their body or limbs; and his accusation is rejected in the divine judgment.
->Colossians 1:13. Revelation 12:10.<-

It emphasizes that while believers still die physically, the legal and spiritual power of death and demonic accusation has been broken.</summary>

->Romans 8:16.<-

311. **Are they then also simultaneously liberated from servile fear?**
Yes, the servile fear <term>servile fear: *knechtische Furcht*, the dread a slave feels toward a master, contrasted with "filial fear" or the loving reverence a child has for a parent</term> of God and His temporal and eternal punishments ceases along with the evil conscience: For perfect love casts out fear. 2 Timothy 1:7; Romans 8:15.
->1 John 4:18.<-

312. **Are they also free from the wrath of God?**
Yes, they are saved through Christ from the wrath, after they have been justified by His blood. Romans 5:9; Isaiah 54:9. <note>original: *Es.* refers to *Esaias*, the German name for the prophet Isaiah.</note>

313. **Are they also free from death?**
Yes, from temporal, spiritual, and eternal death.
->John 8:51.<-

314. **How are they free from temporal death?**
In that they do not have to bear it as a punishment. <note>"Temporal death" refers to physical death—the end of our time on earth.</note>

<summary>This page explores how faith liberates the believer from the rule of sin, the burden of an evil conscience, and the fear of God's punishment. It concludes by introducing the concept that for the believer, physical death is no longer a form of divine punishment.</summary>
<keywords>justification, sanctification, Holy Spirit, servile fear, wrath of God, temporal death, spiritual death, dominion of grace</keywords>

# Of the Blessings of Salvation.

315. **Must not believers die just as well as unbelievers?**
They must indeed die, but death cannot keep them, and they shall live again even though they die: Death is swallowed up in victory, for sin, which is the sting of death, is forgiven them. John 11:25; Isaiah 26:19. <note>original: "Es." is an abbreviation for *Esaias*, the German name for the prophet Isaiah.</note>
->1 Corinthians 15:55, 56.<-

316. **How is the death of believers to be regarded?**
As a gentle sleep and a passing over to the Father. In faith, they fear it as little as a weary person fears their bed of rest.
->Daniel 12:13; Isaiah 57:2; John 11:11.<-
->1 Corinthians 15:20.<-

317. **How have they become free from spiritual death?**
In rebirth <note>Rebirth, or *Wiedergeburt*, refers to the spiritual transformation or "new birth" described in Christian theology.</note>, they have obtained spiritual life through faith in Jesus’ name.
->John 20:31; Question 252.<- <note>original: "Fr. 252" refers to *Frage* (Question) 252 in this same book.</note>

318. **Are they also free from eternal death, hell, and damnation?**
No harm shall come to them from the second death <note>The "second death" is a biblical term from the Book of Revelation referring to final judgment.</note>, for Jesus has redeemed them from the wrath to come.
->1 Thessalonians 1:10; Revelation 2:11.<- <note>original: "Off." is *Offenbarung*, the Book of Revelation.</note>

319. **But are they also free from the devil?**
Yes, they are rescued from the dominion of darkness: He can no longer rule over their understanding or their will, nor their body or their limbs; and his accusation is rejected in the divine judgment.
->Colossians 1:13; Revelation 12:10.<-

<meta>catchword: 320. Are</meta>

<summary>This page explains that while believers still face physical death, it is transformed into a "gentle sleep" through Christ's victory. It also details the believer's liberation from spiritual and eternal death, as well as from the power and accusations of the devil.</summary>
<keywords>salvation, death, sleep, rebirth, second death, victory, devil, dominion of darkness, judgment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Blessings of Salvation." <header>Of the Blessings of Salvation.</header>

### 320. Are they also free from all his snares and temptations?
Not yet, for he still goes about seeking to devour them. 1 Peter 5:8.

### 321. But can they overcome him?
Yes, the Serpent-Treader <note>original: "Schlangen-Treter." This is a poetic title for Jesus Christ, referring to the prophecy in Genesis 3:15 that the offspring of the woman would crush the serpent's head.</note> Jesus Christ has laid the ground for their victory, and they can overcome him through the blood of the Lamb. 1 Moses 3:15 <note>original: "1 Mos." refers to the First Book of Moses, known as Genesis.</note>; Revelation 12:11 <note>original: "Off." refers to *Offenbarung*, the Book of Revelation.</note>.

### 322. With what do they overcome him?
With their spiritual armor through faith, through the Word of God, and prayer, along with spiritual watchfulness. Ephesians 6:13-18; 1 Peter 5:8; 1 John 2:14; Matthew 17:21.

### 323. These are important things from which the believers become free, but what do they now have freedom *for*?
To everything for which the children of God have freedom. Romans 8:21; Question 279 <note>original: "Fr. 279" refers to Question 279 in this same volume.</note>.

### 324. Wherein does this freedom consist?
In a free access to the heavenly Father; freedom for the fellowship of the saints; in all the treasures of salvation in Christ their brother; freedom to use the gifts of the Holy Spirit and His powers for the glory of God; and freedom to possess the heavenly inheritance of eternal glory.

### 325. What else do the believers share in through Jesus Christ?
His Holy Spirit, who is poured out richly upon them in the rebirth <note>Rebirth (*Wiedergeburt*) refers to the spiritual renewal or "being born again" through faith.</note> through Jesus Christ their Savior, so that He may dwell and work within them. Titus 3:5, 6; Romans 8:9, 14.

<summary>This page explains that while believers are still subject to the devil's temptations, they can overcome them through Christ and spiritual discipline. It also details the "positive" freedoms believers gain, including access to God and the indwelling of the Holy Spirit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "Blessings of Salvation," specifically focusing on the role of the Holy Spirit. <header>Of the Blessings of Salvation.</header>

### 326. What treasures of salvation flow to them from the fellowship of the Holy Spirit?
Enlightenment <note>original: "Erleuchtung." In this context, it refers to spiritual illumination, where the Holy Spirit clarifies the mind to understand divine truths.</note>, spiritual joy, and the governance of the Holy Spirit.

### 327. From whom does enlightenment come?
From the Holy Spirit, who enlightens us with His gifts. 1 Corinthians 2:10; Psalm 118:27.

### 328. What part of a person is actually enlightened?
The darkened understanding and the conscience of the person. Ephesians 1:18; chapter 4:18.

### 329. What is imparted to the understanding through enlightenment?
A spiritual capacity to rightly recognize and judge spiritual truths with divine assent <note>original: "Beyfall." This implies an inward "amen" or a deep, spiritual agreement and conviction regarding God's truth.</note>, and to test everything according to the rule of the Word of God. 1 Corinthians 2:15; Romans 12:2; 1 John 4:1; Sirach 9:22 <note>original: "Sir." refers to the Wisdom of Sirach, also known as Ecclesiasticus.</note>.

### 330. Which people are inwardly enlightened by the Spirit of God?
All those who enter into the order of rebirth and renewal.

### 331. Can someone who remains in spiritual death be enlightened?
No, it is said: Arise from the dead, and Christ will enlighten you. Ephesians 5:14.

### 332. What kind of refreshment does the soul enjoy during inward enlightenment?
Spiritual joy, or joy in the Holy Spirit, which is united with true peace. Romans 14:17; Isaiah 61:10 <note>original: "Es." stands for *Esaias*, the German name for the prophet Isaiah.</note>.

It emphasizes that this clarity and the resulting spiritual joy are only accessible to those who have undergone spiritual rebirth.</summary>

### 333. Why do you call it a spiritual joy?
To distinguish it from natural and sinful joy—the kind felt over evil or over the misfortune of other people.

### 334. Who works this sinful joy?
<margin>Ephesians 2:2.</margin>
The evil spirit, who does his work in the children of unbelief.

### 335. Who, however, works spiritual joy?
<margin>1 Thessalonians 1:6.</margin>
The Holy Spirit.

### 336. In what order does He work it?
In the order of repentance and faith, whereby one dies to sin under a godly sorrow <note>original: "göttlichen Traurigkeit." This refers to "godly grief" or "godly sorrow" as described in 2 Corinthians 7:10, which leads to salvation.</note>, recognizes Christ's benefits, and unites oneself with Him.

### 337. What is joy?
A gentle and lovely movement of the soul <note>original: "Gemüths." This term refers to the inner self, encompassing the mind, heart, and emotional disposition of a person.</note>, accompanied by great contentment.

### 338. From where does it arise?
Partly from the enjoyment of present blessings of salvation; partly from the foretaste of future blessings that are kept in heaven.
John 16:24. Hebrews 6:5. Psalm 51:10. Luke 10:20.

### 339. What arises from this joy?
Praise of God; kindness toward one’s neighbor; and a growth in all that is good.
Luke 1:47. Psalm 119:32. Nehemiah 8:10.

### 340. Where will this joy become perfect?
In heaven, where there is fullness of joy and a delightful existence <note>original: "lieblich Wesen." An archaic way of describing the pleasant nature or state of being in God's presence.</note> at the right hand of God, forever and ever. Matthew 25:21. Psalm 16:11.

<summary>This page defines spiritual joy as a gift of the Holy Spirit, distinguishing it from sinful pleasure and explaining its role in the believer's growth and eventual perfection in heaven.</summary>

### 341. What else flows to the believers from the fellowship of the indwelling Holy Spirit?
The governance of the Holy Spirit.
*Psalm* 143, 10. *Galatians* 5, 18.

### 342. In what does the governance of the Holy Spirit consist?
That He performs His office of reproof, teaching, discipline, and comfort <note>The "four offices" (Straf-, Lehr-, Zucht-, and Trost-Amt) are a traditional way of describing how the Holy Spirit works through the Word of God to convict the sinner, teach the truth, discipline the life of the believer, and provide spiritual consolation.</note> in the soul of a child of God.
*Romans* 8, 14. (See above Question 191 and following.)

### 343. How does He govern the understanding?
He enlightens it and convicts it of errors in doctrine and life; He fills it with the knowledge of God and His will; and He prompts it toward the investigation of divine truths.

### 344. How does He govern the will?
He draws the <term>will</term> <note>In this context, the "will" refers to the faculty of the soul that chooses and desires; the text emphasizes that the Spirit must move the will toward the "highest good" (God) because human nature is inclined toward evil.</note> away from evil, awakens it with an inclination of love toward the highest good, and drives it toward the practice of good.

### 345. What does He do to the conscience?
He heals it through the assurance of the forgiveness of sins and of the love of God.

### 346. How should those whom the Holy Spirit governs behave in response?
They should willingly and obediently submit themselves to His soul-saving testimony and instruction, so that they do not hinder His consolations.
*Ephesians* 4, 30.

### 347. Based on all this, in summary, what is the great advantage of believers over unbelievers?

The triune God dwells in them and unites Himself with them, and they therefore enjoy all spiritual strength, life, comfort, and assurance of eternal salvation. *John* 14:23; chapter 17:23; *1 John* 1:3; *Colossians* 1:21–22.

# 5. Concerning the Order of Salvation in Regard to the Duties to be Performed.
<note>The "Order of Salvation" (Latin: *ordo salutis*) is a theological framework used to describe the logical steps or stages in a person's journey from sin to eternal life. Having discussed God's gifts, the text now turns to human responsibility.</note>

## 1.) Of the Conscience.

348. What is the inner guideline of our duties?
**The conscience.**

349. What is the conscience?
It is a judicial testimony or knowledge that God has imprinted into the heart of every human being. <note>In the 18th-century view, the conscience was seen as an innate "internal judge" placed by God in the soul, accessible even to those without the written Bible.</note> *1 Kings* 2:44; *Ecclesiastes* 7:23; *Romans* 1:19.

350. What is the business of the conscience?
It performs a threefold office: 1.) It prescribes laws. 2.) It bears witness to the works that have been done. 3.) It makes judicial pronouncements. <note>The author breaks down the conscience into three roles: a legislator (telling you what to do), a witness (recording what you actually did), and a judge (declaring you innocent or guilty).</note>
*Romans* 2:15; *2 Corinthians* 1:12; *Acts* 23:1.

351. How is the conscience able to carry out these three kinds of business within a person?
It utilizes the **understanding** for recognition and prescription; the **memory** to know and testify to what has happened; and the **will** to approve of or reject the actions. <term>Understanding, Memory, and Will</term> <note>These were considered the three primary faculties of the human soul in traditional Western psychology.</note>

<keywords>Conscience, Order of Salvation, Triune God, Duties, Understanding, Memory, Will, Inner Judge</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the conscience, specifically how its internal operations create a moral obligation for the individual and how the Holy Spirit interacts with it.</meta>

->**Concerning the Conscience.**<-

**352. Does the work of the conscience bind a person to obedience?**

Certainly, for God proves Himself through the conscience as a legislator, witness, and judge. *Romans* 2:15–16.

**353. What is called a "good conscience"?**

That which gives a person a good testimony. Properly speaking, however, a correct and good conscience is one that is so enlightened by the Holy Spirit that it draws its pronouncements from God’s Word, and the person is consciously aware of this. *1 Peter* 3:16, 21; *Job* 27:6; *1 Timothy* 1:5, 19.

**354. Are there not also doubtful, erroneous, and blinded consciences?**

Yes, certainly; but these defects of the conscience stem partly from natural corruption <note>This refers to the theological concept of "original sin," the idea that human nature is inherently flawed or inclined toward error since the Fall of Man.</note>, and partly from habit or becoming hardened in sin. *Hebrews* 10:22.

**355. How may these defects of the conscience be improved?**

When one turns to God from the heart, reads the Holy Scriptures with devotion, and searches for the will of God, then the Holy Spirit will beautifully enlighten and build up the conscience. *Acts* 17:11; *Hebrews* 9:14; *Romans* 9:1.

**356. What, then, is called an "evil conscience"?**

That which gives a person an evil testimony; namely, when one is aware that they have done wrong and deserved punishment. Consequently, one feels the divine wrath in the conscience like a gnawing... 
<note>The sentence concludes on the next page; the catchword indicates the phrase "gnawing worm," a common historical metaphor for the persistent, painful sting of guilt or remorse.</note>

<summary>This page explores the different states of the conscience—good, erroneous, and evil—explaining how the Holy Spirit can enlighten it and how sin can harden it.</summary>
<keywords>conscience, obedience, Good Conscience, Holy Spirit, natural corruption, repentance, Holy Scripture, Evil Conscience, divine wrath</keywords>

->**On the Conscience.**<-

...feels the worm of the soul. <margin>1 Samuel 16:14–15; Wisdom of Solomon 17:10–13; Isaiah 66:24.</margin>

**357. So, must the conscience be viewed differently according to a person’s state?**

<margin>Acts 24:16; Hebrews 13:18.</margin>
Yes; in the <term>State of Grace</term><note>A theological term referring to a life lived under God's forgiveness and favor, usually following conversion.</note>, a person has a correct, pure, peaceful, and joyful conscience.

**358. But what kind of conscience does a person have in the <term>Natural State</term><note>The state of humanity before spiritual awakening or rebirth, often characterized by being "dead in sin."</note>?**

<margin>1 Timothy 4:2; Titus 1:15.</margin>
If he lives in a <term>State of Security</term><note>A spiritual condition where one is dangerously indifferent to sin and feels a false sense of safety from God's judgment.</note>, he has a sleeping, numbed, and <term>seared</term><note>original: "gebrandmarktes"; referring to 1 Timothy 4:2, where a conscience is described as being cauterized or hardened against feeling.</note>, or unfeeling conscience. If he is in a <term>State of Bondage</term><note>A condition where a person is aware of their sin and God's Law but feels only fear and the "slavery" of guilt without the hope of grace.</note>, he has an anxious, fearful, and doubting conscience.

**359. What kind of conscience is it that serves as the inner <term>guideline</term><note>original: "Richtschnur"; literally a "plumb line" or measuring cord used by builders to ensure a wall is straight.</note> for our duties in life?**

<margin>1 Timothy 3:9.</margin>
It is the free, good conscience which a person has in the State of Grace, which is not hindered in its work by the darkness of sin.

**360. There are many different types of duties; how should we categorize them?**

We may appropriately divide them into duties toward **God**; duties toward **ourselves**, and duties toward **other people**.

*Note: Duties are also otherwise called good works.*

header: On Duties.

## 2. Of the Duties toward GOD.

### 361. What then is the reason that we owe a duty to GOD?
Because He is the most excellent Majesty under whom we also stand; and because He has shown us such great, salutary benefits through His works—namely, through <term>Creation and Preservation</term><note>The acts of making the world and sustaining it day-to-day.</note>, and through <term>Redemption and Sanctification</term><note>The acts of saving humanity from sin and making them holy through the Holy Spirit.</note>.

### 362. What are the duties toward GOD generally called?
<term>Divine Service</term><note>original: "Gottesdienst"; while often meaning a church service, here it refers to the broader "service of God" through one's life and devotion.</note>.

### 363. What kind of internal duties belong to Divine Service?
Pure love, <term>childlike fear</term><note>original: "kindliche Furcht"; a theological distinction meaning a fear born of love and respect (like a child for a parent) rather than "servile fear" born of terror or punishment.</note>, humility, adoration, and submission to the will of GOD, heartfelt trust, and hope.

### 364. What kind of external duties belong here?
The calling upon GOD; the proper handling of the Word of GOD; the public confession of faith; and obedience to the commandments of GOD.

### 365. In what does the calling upon GOD consist?
In petitions for the turning away of evil; in prayer for the obtaining of good; in intercession for other people; and in praise or thanksgiving for past benefits. <margin>1 Timothy 2:1.</margin>

<margin>Psalm 50:14–15.</margin>
### 366. What is prayer, or the calling upon GOD?

The Holy Spirit, whom the Heavenly Father will give in the name of Christ to all who ask Him for it; He is a Spirit of prayer, who arranges it properly in our <term>inner being</term><note>original: "Inwendigen"; the internal spiritual life or heart.</note>, as is fitting for us and for **GOD**. ### 3.) Of the Duties toward Ourselves.

373. ->**Do we then also have duties**<-
->**toward ourselves?**<-
Yes, for **GOD** has implanted in all living creatures, and thus also in man, an impulse to seek their well-being; according to this, we should do so as well.

When one avoids and flees everything harmful, but instead strives after the useful good, regarding both soul and body. Sirach 21:2. Jeremiah 4:22.

that everything proceeds honestly and orderly. As well as the <term>simplicity of the mind</term><note>original: "Einfältigkeit des Gemüths"; refers to a humble, sincere, and uncomplicated spirit in one's inner life.</note>, in the use and enjoyment of <term>created things</term><note>original: "Creaturen"; in this context, it refers to all worldly goods, nature, and material resources provided by God.</note>, in food, clothing, and all domestic arrangements.
Matthew 6:19. 1 Timothy 6:8. Chapter 2:9.

**381. What more?**
Patience in <term>adversity</term><note>original: "Widerwärtigkeit"; the trials, hardships, or "crosses" one faces in life.</note>, and no less <term>contentedness</term><note>original: "Vergnügsamkeit"; a state of being satisfied with one's God-given lot in life.</note>, and a proper preparation for a <term>blessed death</term><note>original: "seligen Sterben"; the historical ideal of dying in peace and in a state of grace.</note>. Lamentations 3:26. 1 Timothy 6:6. Sirach 18:22.

### 4.) On Duties Toward Other People.

**382. For what reason are we bound to duties toward other people?**
The fear and love of God binds us to it. It is God's command to us; thus pure fear and love require that we obey Him. Indeed, His <term>example</term><note>original: "Exempel"; specifically the model of character and love provided by Christ.</note> also binds us to it.
Leviticus 19:18. Luke 6:36 <note>original: "seq."; an abbreviation for the Latin *sequens*, meaning "and following."</note>. Matthew 5:45. 1 John 4:21.

**383. By what measure should we guide ourselves in this?**
Well-ordered self-love shall be our guide, for it is said: You shall love your neighbor as yourself. Matthew 7:12. Chapter 22:39.

**384. How might one divide the duties toward other people?**
Into three classes. 1.) Into general duties

...toward all people. 2.) In duties between the different <term>stations</term><note>original: "Ständen"; refers to the various social roles or "estates" (such as family, church, and state) that a person occupied in early modern society.</note> in the world. 3.) In duties regarding all <term>those in need</term><note>original: "Nothleidenden"; literally "those suffering want," encompassing the poor, the sick, and the spiritually distressed.</note>.

### 385. What are the general duties toward all people?

They are intercession, the <term>edification</term><note>original: "Erbauung"; the act of "building up" a neighbor's faith or character through good example and words.</note> of one’s neighbor, helpfulness, peacefulness and reconcilability, patience and humility along with <term>gentleness</term><note>original: "Gelindigkeit"; a soft, mild, or lenient temperament in dealing with others.</note>, as well as friendliness, sincerity, and justice.
->1 Timothy 2:1. Romans 15:2.<-
->Matthew 20:27, 28; chapter 5:9, 23. Colossians 3:12, 13.<-
->Philippians 4:5. Colossians 4:6. Ephesians 4:25. 1 Thessalonians 4:6.<-

### 386. What do you mean by the duties between the different stations?

I mean all proper conduct according to God’s Word and conscience between <term>authorities</term><note>original: "Obrigkeit"; the governing officials or magistracy.</note> and subjects, teachers and listeners, between spouses, parents and children, the old and the young, the rich and the poor, the healthy and the sick.

### 387. What kind of duties belong to the third class?

Toward <term>benefactors</term><note>original: "Wohlthäter"; those who have shown kindness or provided material support.</note>, gratitude is required. Toward the displaced, the tempted, and other miserable people: compassion, counsel, comfort, and help. Toward those who err and toward sinners: <term>brotherly correction</term><note>original: "brüderliche Strafe"; the practice of privately and kindly rebuking a fellow believer for their sins to encourage repentance.</note>.
->Matthew 25:35 and following <note>original: "seq."; abbreviation for the Latin *sequens*.</note>. 3 Moses <note>The Third Book of Moses is the German name for Leviticus.</note> 19:17.<-
->1 Thessalonians 5:14.<-

### 388. What is one obligated to do toward enemies?

We should pray to God for them, that He may forgive them and convert them; and we should, according to our ability, show them kindness in exchange for their evil.
->Matthew 5:44. Romans 12:20.<-

<vocab>**Duties:** moral or religious obligations. **Those in need:** people suffering from poverty or distress. **Intercession:** praying on behalf of others. **Edification:** instructive improvement of the mind or soul. **Humility:** a modest view of one's own importance. **Gentleness:** a mild and kind demeanor. **Authorities:** those in positions of power or government. **Subjects:** those under the rule of an authority. **Benefactors:** people who give help or money to a cause. **Enemies:** those who feel hatred toward or oppose someone.</vocab>

<header>On Good Works.</header>

### 6.) On Good Works.

389. You have recently told me about the duties we owe to God, to ourselves, and to our neighbor: but what do we call the performance and fulfillment of such duties?
They are called **good works**, for it is indeed a good work when one does one’s duty.
->Matthew 5:16. Ephesians 2:10.<-

390. What must be the nature of a work that may rightfully be called a good work?
It must have God’s command before it; it must happen from a faithful, purified, <term>reborn</term><note>original: "wiedergebornen"; referring to the theological concept of spiritual regeneration or being "born again," which was seen as the necessary starting point for any truly holy life.</note> heart; it must be directed toward the glory of God and the benefit of one's neighbor.
->Deuteronomy 12:32. Psalm 119:9. Ezekiel 36:26, 27.<-
->Philippians 1:11. Chapter 2:4.<-

391. So an <term>unregenerate</term><note>original: "unwiedergeborner"; a person who has not yet experienced spiritual rebirth.</note> person cannot do any truly good works?
No, for a <term>corrupt tree</term><note>original: "fauler Baum"; literally a "rotten" or "bad" tree. This follows the biblical metaphor that the internal state of a person determines the moral quality of their actions.</note> can bring forth nothing but evil fruit, but a good tree can bring forth good fruit. Matthew 7:17. Chapter 12:32.

392. From what kind of root and impulse must good works come?
This is faith in Jesus Christ, which possesses a living knowledge of God and His will, and draws the strength for good from Christ’s fullness; and at the same time, love for God, which is the fulfillment of the law. Jeremiah 5:3. Romans 14:23.
->John 1:16. Chapter 14:23. Romans 13:10.<-

<meta>catchword: 393. How</meta>

<summary>This page defines "good works" as the fulfillment of duties toward God and neighbor, specifically stipulating that such works must spring from a "reborn" heart and faith in Jesus Christ to be valid.</summary>

<header>On Good Works.</header>

393. How necessary, then, are good works?
We have been chosen, redeemed, and called by God for this purpose; therefore, those who have come to believe in God must be found in a state of good works. But any tree that does not bring forth good fruit is cut down and thrown into the fire.
->Ephesians 1:4. Titus 2:14. 1 Peter 1:15.<-
->Titus 3:8. Matthew 3:10.<-

394. Are the good works of the <term>reborn</term><note>original: "Wiedergebornen"; those who have experienced spiritual regeneration.</note> then also perfect?
On their part, no, because in this life the evil that still clings to them weakens them, so that they must complain of their <term>insufficiency</term><note>original: "Ungenugsamkeit"; the state of not being enough or failing to meet a standard.</note>. Romans 7:18, 21.

395. Does the heavenly Father also regard their good works as deficient?
No: Because Christ dwells in their hearts through faith, and they are rooted and grounded in Him through love, so their sacrifices or good works are pleasing to Him through Jesus Christ. Ephesians 3:17, Colossians 2:6, 7.
->1 Peter 2:5.<-

396. But how can that be right before God?
Christ, their Head, has already compensated for every <term>deficiency</term><note>original: "Mangelhaftigkeit"; the state of being incomplete or flawed.</note> before the justice of God through His perfect obedience; moreover, He well knows what <term>weak vessels</term><note>original: "schwache Gefässe"; a biblical metaphor (from 1 Peter 3:7 and elsewhere) referring to human fragility and moral weakness.</note> His poor children are. Matthew 5:17. Romans 8:3, 4.
->Psalm 103:13, 14.<-

<meta>catchword: 397. May</meta>

<summary>This page explains that while good works are a necessary fruit of faith, they remain inherently imperfect due to human nature; however, they are accepted by God because of the merit and obedience of Jesus Christ.</summary>
<keywords>good works, spiritual rebirth, imperfection, grace, Jesus Christ, obedience, faith, divine justice</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the "good works" of the reborn and begins a new major section (Chapter V) on the Word of God, the Ministry, and the distinction between the Law and the Gospel. <header>On the Word of God.</header>

397. Can one say: The <term>reborn</term><note>original: "Wiedergebornen"; individuals who have experienced spiritual rebirth or regeneration.</note> are able to keep God’s commandments?
Yes; according to their new nature, which is born of God, they do not sin and they keep His commandments. For God works in them both the willing and the doing according to His good pleasure.
->1 John 3:9. Chapter 5:3. Philippians 2:13.<-

398. Do they earn anything before God through their good works?
Nothing; they only do their <term>duty</term><note>original: "Schuldigkeit"; an obligation or what is owed.</note>, in that they are God’s <term>instruments</term><note>original: "Geschirre"; literally "vessels" or "tools," emphasizing that the believer is a secondary cause while God is the primary actor.</note>, who provides them with the impulse, the strength, and the opportunity for it, and thus works through them Himself. ->Luke 17:10.<-
->Ephesians 2:10. Romans 11:35, 36. John 15:5.<-

399. But does God still intend to reward them abundantly?
Yes, exceedingly abundantly; however, the basis of the reward rests on the rich goodness and grace of the Heavenly Father, and it comes according to justice because of the merit of Jesus Christ and the union of faith with Him. ->Romans 2:6, 7.<-
->Matthew 20:15.<-

-><note>original: "Offenb. 12, 11"; Revelation 12:11.</note><-

### 401. What is the living, eternal Word of God?

It is the only Son of God, through whom God created everything and revealed Himself; who in the <term>fullness of time</term><note>A biblical expression referring to the moment in history when God decided to send Jesus to Earth.</note> became flesh, through whom also all things (but especially the Church of God) are sustained and governed.
-><note>original: "Joh. 1, 1. ſeq. 14"; John 1:1–14. Revelation 19:11–13.</note><-

### 402. Why is this Word called "single"?

Because, properly speaking, God—according to the characteristic of His spiritual essence—has, speaks, and utters no other Word. <note>John 4:24. Revelation 19:13.</note>

### 403. What might it signify that the Son of God is called "the Word"?

This name leads us to the attribute of <term>revelation</term><note>The act of God making Himself known to humanity.</note> in God: through the Word, the hidden divine essence has revealed itself—namely, that He **Is**, together with His nature and His will—just as we reveal ourselves through our own word.
-><note>Matthew 11:27. Galatians 1:16.</note><-

### 404. How has God revealed Himself through this Word of His?

God has revealed Himself through it in both an outward and an inward manner, gloriously and lovely.
-><note>original: "4 Moſ. 12, 6. 7. 8"; Numbers 12:6–8.</note><-

<header>On the Word of God.</header>

### 405. How has He revealed Himself in an outward manner?

First, when God established the entire creation through His almighty speaking; by which He showed how great, almighty, wise, and kind He is.
-><note>original: "1 Mos. 1, 3. Pf. 33, 6. 9."; Genesis 1:3; Psalms 33:6, 9.</note><-

### 406. How even more so?

Through the Word of testimony, especially the Holy Scripture—namely, when the eternal Word <note>Referring back to Christ as the Word, as defined in Question 401.</note> revealed God’s essence, attributes, <term>counsel</term><note>God's hidden plans or purposes for humanity.</note>, word, and will even more to mankind (and continues to do so) through human speech, voice, and writing in His own people.
-><note>original: "Jes. 34, 16. Col. 1, 25."; Isaiah 34:16; Colossians 1:25.</note><-

### 407. How does God reveal Himself through His Word in an inward manner?

When He, according to His spiritual nature, speaks spiritually to the soul: that is, that He enlightens the understanding, warms the will, and creates the person anew inwardly—which He can also do in infant children and ignorant <term>heathens</term><note>A historical term for those who have not yet been reached by Christian teaching.</note>.
-><note>original: "Pf. 85, 9. Hohel. 5, 2. Matth. 16, 17."; Psalms 85:8 (85:9 in German Bibles); Song of Solomon 5:2; Matthew 16:17.</note><-

### 408. It seems to me you mean to say: there is one single Word of God, but it teaches in two different ways?

Yes, that is what I mean; this single Word teaches us, as already stated, in an outward and an inward manner. Therefore, it is also said that there is an "inner" and an "outer" Word.

### 409. What is understood by the "outer word"?

The testimony of the Word of God from without,

### 410. Is such testimony then not also to be called the Word of God?

<margin><note>original: "1 Thess. 2, 13."</note> 1 Thessalonians 2:13.</margin>
Yes, it is not the word of man, but it is the Word of God, because God gives it, and it teaches nothing other than what is eternal, constant truth before God.

### 411. But is it not still taught through men?

<margin><note>original: "1 Cor. 3, 5."</note> 1 Corinthians 3:5.</margin>
Yes, but men are not the authors of it; they only offer up their heart, voice, and hand for its teaching, and serve it so that it may come forth.

### 412. What is the difference between the inner and the outer word?

The inner word is spoken and taught by God Himself; the outer word is spoken and taught by men.

### 413. Is it then necessary to take note of such a distinction regarding the Word of God?

<margin><note>original: "2 Cor. 11, 13."</note> 2 Corinthians 11:13.</margin>
In these times it is quite necessary, because through the cunning of the old serpent <note>A traditional reference to the Devil's deception.</note> many dare to speak the Word of God who have not received it from the Lord, and yet they wish to attribute divine power for <term>salvation</term><note>German: "Seligkeit"; refers to eternal happiness or the state of being saved.</note> to their own word.

### 414. In such abuse, is the power of the inner word not present?

<margin><note>original: "2 Cor. 6, 14. 15."</note> 2 Corinthians 6:14, 15.</margin>
No, for Christ is never at one with the perverse generation <note>The author likely refers to those who preach without true divine calling or spiritual alignment.</note>.

When the Word of God is not falsified, but spoken as God’s Word; when it is spoken out of sincerity, and from God, before God in Christ, and when it agrees with Holy Scripture. *1 Peter 4:11; 2 Corinthians 2:17, 4:2; 2 Timothy 1:13, 14; Acts 26:22; Titus 2:1.*

->416. Who is capable of being a<-
->preacher of the Gospel in such a manner?<-
One is capable whom the Lord makes capable for it, calls, and sends. *2 Corinthians 3:5, 6; Acts 20:28; John 20:21.*

->present in such preaching<-
->of the Gospel?<-
Certainly, for the divine truth (as the inner word) teaches there through His servant, and the listener is reached by its power from within and without. <note>The "inner word" refers to the Holy Spirit's direct work on the soul, while the "outer word" is the spoken sermon.</note> *Luke 10:16.*

->418. Can one also have thorough testimony<-
->of such a wholesome dealing?<-
Yes indeed, for the messengers of the Gospel are called God’s fellow-workers, with whom the Lord works, and He has promised to be with them in this way until the end of the world. <note>The "wholesome dealing" refers to the spiritual transaction of preaching and receiving the Word.</note> *1 Corinthians 3:9; Mark 16:20; Acts 11:21; Matthew 28:20.*

->of God produce when it is so rightly preached<-
->and heard?<-

### 420. If this is so, why then are not all listeners made whole and blessed?

Because such people separate the <term>transaction</term><note>German: "Handel"; here referring to the spiritual process of hearing and receiving the Word. ->Acts 28:26, 27.<-

It approaches the <term>soul</term><note>German: "Gemüthe"; refers to the inner disposition, mind, and feeling of a person.</note> of the listener from the outside, through the ears and eyes; it proclaims the will of God to them, and offers and commends to them the grace and power of God unto life.
->Acts 2:14, chapter 20:27.<-

### 422. What, at the same time, is the actual function of the inner word?

As Spirit, it acts in a spiritual manner upon the spiritual part of the person; there it powerfully presses in as light and life upon the understanding and the will, with the truth that the <term>service-word</term><note>German: "Dienst-Wort"; the audible word preached by a human servant/minister, as opposed to the "inner word" spoken by the Holy Spirit.</note> testifies to; thus it works and strengthens faith. Acts 2:37; Luke 24:32.

I hold that it is the infallible Word

### 424. Where does the Holy Scripture have its origin?

It was written down by holy men of God through the power of the eternal Word, by the impulse and through the inspiration of the Holy Spirit.
->2 Peter 1:20, 21; Hebrews 1:1.<-

### 425. How should we rightly prepare ourselves for reading and hearing such precious witness of divine revelation?

Out of proper reverence, we should be very attentive to its teaching in order to perceive it; turn to God with <term>heartfelt sighs</term><note>German: "herzlichem Seufzen"; a common Pietist expression for inward, sincere prayer or longing for God's guidance.</note>, for He wishes to teach us here; preserve the teaching in our hearts; and form a resolution to obey the recognized will of God. Hebrews 2:1; 2 Peter 1:19; Matthew 13:23, chapter 7:24.

### 426. Should one not also note a particular distinction regarding the word of witness?

Yes, for there is a word of the Law and a word of the Gospel. John 1:17.

### 427. Which is the word of the Law?

They are the commandments and statutes that God gave through Moses to the people of Israel, as they are to be read in the books of Moses. Deuteronomy 4:1, 2.

### 424. Where does Holy Scripture originate from?

<margin>2 Peter 1:20, 21; Hebrews 1:1.</margin>
It has been written down by holy men of God through the power of the eternal Word, by the impulse and through the inspiration of the Holy Spirit.

### 425. How should we properly prepare ourselves for reading and hearing such a precious testimony of divine revelation?

<margin>Hebrews 2:1; 2 Peter 1:19; Matthew 13:23; chapter 7:24.</margin>
We should be very attentive to its teaching out of due reverence in order to perceive it; turn to God with <term>heartfelt sighing</term><note>German: "herzlichem Seufzen"; a common Pietist expression for inward, wordless prayer or yearning for God's guidance.</note>, He who wishes to teach us here; preserve the teaching in our hearts; and form a resolution to obey the recognized will of God.

### 426. Should one not also note a particular distinction regarding the word of testimony?

<margin>John 1:17.</margin>
Yes, for there is a word of the <term>Law</term><note>Latin: "Gesetzes"; referring to the divine commands.</note> and a word of the <term>Gospel</term><note>Latin: "Evangelii"; referring to the "good news" of grace.</note>.

### 427. Which is the word of the Law?

<margin>Deuteronomy 4:1, 2.</margin>
They are the commandments and statutes which God gave to the people of Israel through Moses, as the same may be read in the Books of Moses.

429. **How are these two distinguished?**

The former is called by Paul the <term>Old Testament</term><note>Referring here to the Mosaic Law and the "letter" that convicts.</note>, also called the "letter"; the latter he calls the <term>New Testament</term><note>Referring to the era of grace and the internal work of the Spirit.</note>, also called "spirit." The former is a <term>doctrine of works</term><note>German: "Werk-Lehre"; the teaching that emphasizes human effort and obedience to the Law.</note>; the latter is a <term>doctrine of faith</term><note>German: "Glaubens-Lehre"; the teaching that emphasizes salvation as a gift received through faith.</note>. The former presents God to us as an angry judge; the latter as a reconciled God and dear Father.
<margin>2 Corinthians 3:14; Romans 4:5; Galatians 3:12.</margin>

430. **What, then, is the office or function of the Law?**

It teaches and commands what man should do, namely how he should live righteously and innocently; and it proclaims to transgressors the curse and wrath of God.
<margin>5 Moses <note>The "Fifth Book of Moses" is the German name for Deuteronomy.</note> 6:1; chapter 27:26.</margin>
->(See Questions 107–109.)<-

431. **How does man find himself under such teaching of the Law?**

He finds himself under condemnation, because sin has so corrupted him that he finds no power to fulfill the Law, and thus no way to escape the curse.
<margin>Galatians 3:10; Romans 7:18; chapter 3:20.</margin>

432. **What, then, is the office or function of the Gospel?**

It teaches and extols what God has done for us; reveals the <term>righteousness of faith</term><note>The theological concept that a person is made right with God through faith in Christ rather than their own merits.</note>; leads the sorrowful sinner to Christ; and offers him God’s grace and power for a new, holy life.
<margin>2 Corinthians 5:18–21; Romans 3:21; chapter 6:14.</margin>

## VI. On the Institutions of Christ, which are commonly called **Sacraments**, namely **Baptism and the Lord's Supper**.

434. **What kind of public actions did the Lord JESUS command his disciples, in addition to the Ministry of the Word?**
**Baptism** and the **Lord’s Supper**. <note>German: "Abendmal"; literally "Evening Meal," the standard term for Holy Communion or the Eucharist.</note>

435. **Of what parts do these actions consist?**
They both consist, each one, of two essential parts: namely, a physical, visible part, and a spiritual, invisible part. Both have the same kind of utility <note>Or "benefit"; they both serve the same purpose of strengthening faith.</note>, yet each in its own order.

436. **What do you mean by that?**
The physical, visible part occurs to the outward part of the person; the spiritual, invisible part takes place within his inward part.

437. **What is the physical, visible part that occurs at Baptism?**

<summary>This page introduces the Sacraments (Baptism and the Lord's Supper) as "Institutions of Christ," explaining that they consist of both an outward physical ceremony and an inward spiritual transformation.</summary>

->On the Institutions of Christ.<-

The body is bathed with water by the servant of the Lord.
<margin>Acts 8:38.</margin>

### 438. What is the invisible process that occurs therein?

The soul is cleansed from sins with the blood of Christ and the water of grace, through the Holy Spirit.
<margin>1 Corinthians 6:11.</margin>

### 439. What is the visible action during the Lord’s Supper?

The assembled Christians are fed and given to drink with bread and wine at the table of the Lord. <note>German: "Abendmal"; literally "Evening Meal," the common term for Holy Communion.</note>
<margin>1 Corinthians 10:16.</margin>

### 440. What is the invisible action?

The souls of Christian believers hereby enjoy the food and drink of the body and blood of Christ, which their Heavenly High Priest <note>A reference to Jesus Christ, particularly as described in the Book of Hebrews.</note> distributes to them. <margin>Genesis 14:18. <note>The original text uses "Mos." (Moses) to refer to the Pentateuch; Genesis 14:18 describes Melchizedek bringing out bread and wine.</note> John 6:27, 51.</margin>

### 441. Must both of these parts then be present and enjoyed in Baptism and the Lord’s Supper?

Yes, for so the Lord has instituted it; and where one of the two is missing, it is not an action of His institution.

### 442. Can the servants of Christ then bring forth the spiritual part at the same time as the physical?

No, but the Lord who commanded it intends to be present Himself when His command is followed in the obedience of faith.
<margin>Matthew 28:20.</margin>

# On Baptism.

It is for each person themselves, and at the same time among and towards one another, a confession, token, and testimony of the participation in the internal benefit indicated thereby, in heart, soul, and conscience.
<margin>Romans 4:11. 2 Moses 13:9. <note>The German text uses "2 Moses" to refer to the book of Exodus.</note></margin>

### 444. What else is linked even more with it?
The bond of brotherly love among one another; the strengthening of faith, to lift their hearts to the love of Christ and heartfelt thanksgiving for His redemptive benefits.

## 1. On Baptism.

### 445. When did the Lord Jesus institute baptism?
After His resurrection, before He ascended to heaven, when He sent His disciples out into the world to preach the Gospel. <margin>Mark 16:15.</margin>

### 446. So does baptism belong to the proper administration of the Gospel?
Yes, that is evident from this.

### 447. With what words did the Lord command the disciples to baptize?
He spoke to them: All authority is given to me in heaven and on earth; therefore go out and teach all nations, and baptize them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit. <margin>Matthew 28:18, 19.</margin>

### 448. Who was meant to baptize?
The disciples sent by the Lord, or the servants of Christ.

# On Baptism.

449. **Who should be baptized?**
All those who have been instructed in the faith and have become believers. <note>Acts (Ap. Gesch.) 14:21; Mark 16:16.</note>

450. **How could they know who**
->**were believers or not?**<-
Those who wished to be baptized were required to confess their faith, for the faith of the heart and the confession of the mouth belong together. <note>Acts (Ap. Gesch.) 8:36–38; chapter 10:46, 47; Romans 10:9, 10.</note>

451. **Why must the candidates for baptism first**
->**be instructed and believe?**<-
Faith for confession comes from hearing the word of the Gospel: but whoever hears and does not believe does not belong to the <term>congregation of Christ</term> <note>German: *Gemeine Christi*, referring to the community of believers or the church</note>, and will be condemned. <note>Romans 10:14, 17; John 3:18; Mark 16:16.</note>

452. **How should the baptism be performed?**
The believing <term>candidate for baptism</term> <note>original: *Täufling*</note>, having confessed their faith in the name of the triune God, shall be bathed with water by the baptizer in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. <note>Acts (Ap. Gesch.) 22:16.</note>

453. **What then is such an <term>act of baptism</term>**
->**to the candidate?**<-
<unclear>He</unclear> <insert><unclear>gives</unclear></insert> <note>original: *Er giebet*</note> thereby a confession, token, and testimony of the received internal purification and washing away of sins through faith. <note>1 Peter 3:21; 1 Corinthians 6:11.</note>

454. **How does faith occupy itself**
->**during such an act of baptism, toward**<-
->**internal purification?**<-

# On Baptism..

In the immersion <note>original: *Ueberschwemmung*, literally 'flooding' or 'inundation,' referring here to the water covering the person</note> of the water, he sees and grasps the drowning of the <term>old man</term> <note>The "old man" or "old self" refers to the sinful nature that Christians believe is put to death through union with Christ.</note> in the death of Christ, and commits himself to it.
->Colossians 2:12. Romans 6:3.<-

455. **What does faith do upon coming**
->**forth out of the water?**<-

There it sees and leads into the fellowship of the resurrection of JESUS Christ to a new life, and commits itself to it. Romans 6:4, 11. 1 Peter 3:21.

456. **Is baptism a confession of**
->**these great mysteries?**<-

Yes, therein lies the reason that the participation through faith in the death and resurrection of JESUS Christ—for liberation from sins and for the power of a new life—is thereby freely and publicly confessed.
->Acts <note>original: *Apost. Gesch.*</note> 2:38.<-

457. **In what manner is Christian**
->**baptism received as a token and**<-
->**testimony?**<-

Because the <term>candidate for baptism</term> <note>original: *Täufling*</note> is thereby received into the assembled <term>congregation</term> <note>original: *Gemeine*, the community of believers</note> of Christ, it is granted to him by Christ's command from the congregation through baptism, and testimony is given of the fellowship as a member under and in Christ.
->Acts 10:47. 1 Corinthians 12:13.<-

458. **What is baptism to him according**
->**to its internal part?**<-

It is to him a bath of rebirth and renewal of the Holy Spirit, through which he <term>puts on Christ</term> <note>original: *Christum anzeucht*, an archaic form of *anzieht*, meaning to clothe oneself in Christ</note> and becomes a child of God.
->Titus 3:5. Galatians 3:27.<-

## On the Lord’s Supper.

461. **When did the Lord JESUS institute the Lord’s Supper?** <note>original: *Abendmal*, literally "evening meal," the term used in German for Holy Communion or the Eucharist.</note>

In the night when he was betrayed. 1 Corinthians 11:23.

462. **How did he begin?**

He took bread into his hands.

463. **What did he do next?**

He gave thanks to his dear Heavenly Father. 1 Corinthians 11:24.

464. **What did he do with the bread?**

He broke it in two <note>original: *entzwey*, meaning into pieces or asunder.</note>.

465. **Why did he do this?**

So that everyone might receive some of it, and so that he might simultaneously represent the breaking of his body on the cross. John 2:19.

466. **What did he do with the broken bread?**

He gave it to his disciples, to show that he is the giver and distributor of the <term>Bread of Life</term> <note>A title Jesus uses for himself in the Gospel of John, signifying that he is the source of spiritual nourishment.</note>. John 6:27.

# On the Lord’s Supper.

467. **What should the disciples do with the given bread?**
He said: Take, eat.

468. **What did he wish to teach spiritually by this?**
That believers should reach out for the <term>Bread of Life</term> <note>A title Jesus gives himself in the Gospel of John, indicating he is the essential source of spiritual life.</note>, open their <term>mouth of faith</term> <note>original: *Glaubens-Mund*. A common Pietist metaphor suggesting that just as the physical mouth receives bread, the "mouth" of the soul—which is faith—receives spiritual grace.</note> toward it, and take it into themselves. <margin>John 6:27.</margin>

469. **What did he say next?**
He said: This is my body, which is broken for you.
<margin>1 Corinthians 11:24.</margin>

470. **What did he wish to teach with this?**
He wished to teach: My body is to the soul what eaten bread is to the body.

471. **What is eaten bread to the body?**
It is a powerful food and nourishment for the preservation of natural life.

472. **Is the body of Christ then that, or such a thing, to the soul?**
Yes, he says it himself: My flesh is the true food. <note>original: *die rechte Speise*, meaning the proper, true, or right nourishment.</note> <margin>John 6:55.</margin>

473. **What did the HErr wish to point to with the little word This?**
To the then-present action of giving and eating the bread which feeds the body; exactly that is what they should expect from his body when he gave it to them and they ate it—namely, that it feeds the soul. <margin>John 6:51.</margin>

474. **How can you be certain of such a meaning from these words?**
The little word **Is** assures me of it,

# On the Lord’s Supper.

### 475. What kind of emphasis does the little word **Is** have here?

The little word **Is** (especially when Eternal Truth <note>A title used here for Jesus Christ, implying that his words are the ultimate reality.</note> speaks it from his own mouth) always determines that the condition is essentially and truly as it is being testified.

### 476. What do you wish to prove by this?

That the body of Christ is essentially and truly that which he intended to show us with the preceding action—namely, a true food, or what bread and eating are to the physical body.

### 477. But perhaps the Lord intended to speak of the bread with the word **is**?

That cannot be, for it can be proven neither through Scripture nor through the nature of the matter that baked bread is, can be, or unites itself essentially with the body of Christ. <note>This reflects a common Protestant theological distinction: while the bread is used in the ritual, the bread itself does not literally become the physical flesh of Christ in a biological sense, but rather serves a spiritual function.</note>

->Matthew 24:23, Luke 17:23.<-

### 478. What does the little word **my** remind us of?

That we should take note: the <term>God-Man</term> <note>original: *GOtt-Mensch*. A theological term emphasizing that Jesus is simultaneously fully divine and fully human.</note> is speaking here of his own human body.

### 479. What are we to learn from the word **Body**?

That we should know and believe that Christ’s body truly possesses the quality of providing nourishment, and also that he wishes to give it to us as food.

->John 6:55, 27.<-

### 480. What do the words, **which is broken for you**, teach us?

# On the Lord’s Supper.

...words that he does not speak of the state of his body as he sat there before them, but as he would be when he would be perfected through suffering, death, and resurrection at the right hand of God. <note>The text suggests that the body Christ gives in the sacrament is his glorified, heavenly body, rather than his mortal body as it existed before the crucifixion.</note>
->John 6:51, Hebrews 5:9.<-

### 481. Is it then necessary to believe that Christ’s body is food, and that one must also eat it?

Yes, very necessary, for he declares and says: Truly, truly, I say to you, if you do not eat the flesh of the Son of Man and drink his blood, you have no life in you.
->John 6:53.<-

### 482. But how can he (Christ) give us his flesh to eat?

On our part, it does not happen with a physical mouth or eating; and on Christ’s part, it is not according to the state in which he was visible here, for in that state the flesh is of no use for food. <note>This reflects the teaching in John 6:63: "It is the spirit that gives life; the flesh is of no use at all." The author argues against a literal, physical consumption of Christ's mortal body.</note>
->John 6:52, 63; John 12:24.<-

### 483. If it does not happen that way, how does it happen then?

It happens in the state of Christ’s flesh where he has ascended to where he was before, where his flesh has been glorified <note>original: *verkläret*. This refers to the transfiguration or transformation into a divine, heavenly state.</note> through the Spirit of eternal divinity and has become spiritual; there, it feeds and nourishes our spirit for life, and is thus eaten spiritually through faith.
->John 6:62, 63, 47; John 17:5; 1 Corinthians 15:45.<-

### 484. Did the Lord celebrate the Lord’s Supper only with bread?

No, he also gave the disciples wine to drink.

# On the Lord’s Supper.

### 485. How do you know that?

The Evangelists and Paul testify that the Lord also took a cup with wine, gave thanks, gave it to them, and commanded them to drink from it; and they all drank from it. 
->Matthew 26:27; Mark 14:23; 1 Corinthians 11:25.<-

### 486. What did the Lord say when they had drunk the wine?

He said: This is my blood of the New Testament <note>The German word *Testament* can mean both a "will" and a "covenant." In this biblical context, it refers to the New Covenant between God and humanity.</note> which is shed for many for the forgiveness of sins; or, This cup is the New Testament in my blood, which is shed for you.
->Matthew 26:28; Mark 14:24; Luke 22:20; 1 Corinthians 11:25.<-

### 487. How do you understand these words?

Just like the previous ones; namely, that Christ’s shed (now glorified) <note>original: *verklärtes*. This refers to the state of Christ's body and blood after the resurrection, suggesting they are no longer merely physical but divine and spiritual.</note> blood is a drink; it is to the soul what drunk wine is to the body.

### 488. What benefit comes from drinking the blood of Christ?

Whoever drinks it has forgiveness of sins; he becomes and is a partner in the covenant <note>original: *Bundesgenosse*. This implies someone who shares in the rights and promises of a treaty or alliance with God.</note> of the New Testament.

### 489. What did the Lord say further?

Do this, as often as you drink it, in remembrance of me; a command which he also gave regarding the handling of the bread.
->1 Corinthians 11:24, 25.<-

### 490. What kind of words are these?

They are the words of institution <note>The "Words of Institution" are the specific phrases spoken by Jesus at the Last Supper, which are repeated by clergy during the consecration of the bread and wine in a church service.</note>, with which he com—

<summary>This page explains the significance of the wine in the Lord's Supper, identifying it as Christ's blood of the New Covenant which provides forgiveness of sins. It emphasizes that this spiritual nourishment is received through faith, mirroring the soul's needs to the body's physical thirst.</summary>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Words of Institution" for the Lord's Supper. The previous page ended with the beginning of the word "commands" (befiehlet), explaining that Christ ordered his disciples to repeat his actions.</meta>

commands that his disciples should practice a similar action among themselves.

### 491. What does the word "this" point to?
<note>The German text uses *Solches* (such/this thing). The author is analyzing the specific phrasing of "Do this in remembrance of me."</note>
Just like the word "that," it points back to the action <note>original: *Handel*. In this context, it refers to the ritual act or "dealing" with the elements.</note> which he had undertaken and practiced there with bread and wine.

### 492. Do you have anything further to note regarding the word "this"?
Yes, it confirms the indicated understanding of the word "that," because it is found that the same "that" and here the "this" are exactly one and the same word in its original language. <note>The author is referring to the Greek word *touto*, which serves as the basis for both "This is my body" and "Do this." He argues that if one refers to the ritual action, the other must as well.</note>

### 493. What conclusion do you draw from this?
I conclude thus: Just as it is fair and right that I may read "this" <note>original: *solches*</note> here, with equal fairness and right I may also read there: "This is my body" and "this is my blood"; thus the indicated meaning of the Lord is clearly found once again.

### 494. To what purpose should the Lord’s Supper be held?
The Lord said: Do this in remembrance of me. <margin>1 Corinthians 11:24.</margin>

### 495. In what way should they especially remember him in this?
Principally his incomprehensible love and benefit, shown to them through his most bitter death on the cross.

# On the Lord's Supper.

->1 Corinthians 11:26.<-

### 497. Why then is the remembrance of his death particularly fitting here?
Because they should deal with the food and drink for the body for the very purpose that they might perceive together the benefit <note>original: *Nußbarkeit*. ->1 Corinthians 10:17.<-

### 498. How do you derive the memory of Christ’s death from this?
Look, this blessed benefit was prepared for us because Christ, out of heartfelt love, gave himself into death for us and shed his blood for us, so that he might refresh <note>original: *laben*. A word often used for quenching thirst or providing cooling comfort.</note> us with it.
->John 15:13.<-

### 499. How would it go for us if we did not have this soul-nourishment ready?
We would all have to waste away in eternal death-hunger.
->John 6:53.<-

He has eternal life; Christ will also raise his body on the Last Day <note>original: *jüngsten Tage*. Literally "the youngest day," a common German term for Judgment Day or the resurrection at the end of time.</note> to eternal life; indeed, Christ has thereby united himself so closely with him that he remains in Christ and Christ in him.
->John 6:54, 56, 57.<-

## VII. Of the Three Principal Estates, namely:

1. Of the Christian Church.
2. Of the Secular Authority <note>original: *weltlichen Obrigkeit*. This refers to the civil government or ruling powers responsible for maintaining earthly order.</note>.
3. Of the State of Matrimony.

### 1. Of the Christian Church.

**507. What do you profess concerning the Christian Church?**
I believe that it is the number and assembly of all chosen children of God, who are also called the body and the bride of Christ.

**508. Why is the Church called an assembly?**
Because all its members are gathered by the one Shepherd, Christ, into a spiritual flock; indeed, because together, under and in the one Head, they are one body and have one Spirit of faith.
John 10:14, 16. Ephesians 4:15, 16; chapter 4:4.

### 510. May they then also properly be called children of GOD?
Yes, for the Heavenly Father has ordained them out of His own good pleasure to sonship toward Himself, and has also fathered <note>original: *gezeuget* — also meaning 'begotten' or 'generated'</note> them through His word of truth. Ephesians 1:5. 1 John 3:1. James 1:18.

### 511. Are they not already GOD’S children by nature?
Oh no; by nature they are children of wrath and of Satan, according to his image and kind, like the others. Ephesians 2:3. John 8:44.

They become children of God in the **rebirth** <note>original: *Wiedergeburt*. Ephesians 2:8. John 1:12. Galatians 4:5.

### 513. Why is it that the Church is called the body of Christ?
Because all its members are attached and incorporated into Christ the Head through faith, so that under Him, together, they constitute **One spiritual body**. Colossians 1:18. Ephesians 4:15, 16. Romans 12:4, 5.

### 514. Why is this congregation also called the bride of Christ?
For this reason: because the most intimate love, union, and fellowship prevail between Christ and the same.

-># Of the Christian Church.<-

### 520. May hypocrites or the ungodly not also be members of this church, or mingle among them?

By no means! No one can be a member of this Church of God unless he is a **living member** <note>original: *lebendiges Glied*. In this context, it distinguishes between those who simply attend services and those whose faith is active and spiritually "alive."</note> of Christ, such that Christ counts him as His own; for this number is not according to man’s counting, but according to God’s. Isaiah 60:21. John 15:6. Matthew 7:23.

### 521. Who, then, gathers this number together and brings them to such unity?

The Head, Christ, does this through the office and the power of His **Holy Spirit**. Isaiah 49:22; 60:4, 21. Ephesians 2:21.

### 522. From what kind of people and place does He gather them?

He gathers them from all peoples, and from all places and ends of the earth: For it is a **universal church** <note>original: *allgemeine Kirche*. Historically, this refers to the "catholic" or whole church, meaning it is not restricted to one nation or physical location.</note>. Isaiah 43:5, 7. Revelation 5:9; 7:9.

### 523. How long does the gathering last?

It lasts from the beginning of the world until the **Last Day** <note>original: *jüngsten Tage*. Refers to the final judgment or the end of time.</note>. Hebrews 11:16. Matthew 28:20.

### 524. What kind of gathering is this; can we also see it with our eyes and point to it with our fingers?

No; since we have been speaking thus far of the Church as it stands before God, its gathering happens in the Spirit, spiritually, and is therefore manifest only before the eyes of God; but on the Last Day it will also [be manifest...]

### 525. Has then the dear **God** not also an externally gathered church upon His earth?
Yes, for He has had such a church first among the Patriarchs, thereafter among the people of Israel, and finally among the Christian people.

### 526. What does **God** usually call such an externally gathered church?
He often and frequently calls it His **vineyard**, which He Himself has planted with great diligence and faithfulness. Isaiah 5:1, 2, 7. Mark 12:1. Psalm 80:16.

### 527. Does **God** not still desire to have a beautiful, externally gathered Christian church?
Yes, certainly He desires to have a gathered Christian people, which—like a city situated on a mountain—shines gloriously before all the world in pure doctrine and in the order of faith and life, in which He may see His delight. <note>The "city on a mountain" is a reference to Matthew 5:14, suggesting that the church should be visible to others through its conduct and teaching.</note> Isaiah 27:2, 3; 62:3, 4, 5. Matthew 5:14.

### 528. By what means does the **Lord Christus** gather for Himself a people on earth, which is now called the Christian Church?
Through the word of His **Gospel** <note>original: *Evangelii*. The author uses the Latin form of "Gospel."</note>.
Mark 16:16. Colossians 1:6. Acts 14:21.

### 529. How does this come to pass?
He gives teachers after His heart, who proclaim His name to the people, so that they may be converted and sanctified through faith in **Jesus**.
