### 530. **How does the Lord Jesus act during such preaching of the Gospel?**
He is present Himself with His servants, and grants His Spirit and blessing to the Word. <note>original: *Evangelii*. This refers back to the "Gospel" mentioned on the previous page.</note> Matthew 28:20.

### 531. **What then occurs among the listeners?**
In those who give the Word a proper hearing, the Holy Spirit kindles faith and the love of **God**, whereby they are awakened and drawn toward true worship, toward love for one another, and toward obedience in all godliness. Acts 16:14, 15. Romans 12:10. 1 Peter 1:22.

### 532. **What follows further from such a good foundation?**
An external fellowship among and with one another, arising from heartfelt faithfulness. Acts 2:44.

### 533. **In what sorts of things do they maintain an external fellowship?**
In general, they seek to serve one another in everything that is good; but they specifically bind themselves to one another for common Christian discipline <note>The word "discipline" (*Zucht*) here refers to the shared rules of conduct and mutual accountability within a religious community.</note> and for communal worship. 1 Peter 4:10. Acts 2:42. 1 Corinthians 1:10.

### 534. **What do you understand here by communal worship?**
Where the believers assemble for the proclamation and hearing of the Word; for common prayer, praise, and thanksgiving before **God**; and for the administration of the ordinances of Christ. <note>original: *Einsetzungen Christi*. This refers to the sacred rites established by Jesus, specifically Baptism and the Lord's Supper.</note> Acts 10:33.

-># Of the Christian Church.<-

### 535. **To whom, however, does the supervision over such an external fellowship particularly belong?**
The supervision belongs to the teachers, elders, and overseers; <note>original: *Vorstehern*. These were lay leaders or administrators who managed the practical affairs of the congregation.</note> they are to watch over the flock of Christ and provide them with sound doctrine and discipline. <note>original: *Zucht*. In this historical context, "discipline" refers to the shared rules of conduct and mutual accountability that kept the community in line with their faith.</note> Acts 20:28. Isaiah 62:6. Titus 1:5.

### 536. **What is one to think of such a Christian Church?**
That they are the people of Christ’s own possession, members of the invisible Church before **God**; and that to them belong all the good promises of **God**, both in the kingdom of grace <note>The "kingdom of grace" refers to the present life of a believer on earth.</note> as well as hereafter in the kingdom of glory. 1 Peter 2:9. Galatians 3:14, 29.

### 537. **Do not hypocrites and false ones perhaps run among this Christian assembly as well?**
Yes, such people do mingle among them, though they never truly belong to the foundation: however, the Lord will finally sweep them away as weeds and chaff from His field and threshing floor. <note>original: *Unkraut und Spreu*. This is a biblical metaphor for people whose faith is not genuine, who will be separated from the "wheat" (true believers) at the final judgment.</note> Matthew 13:25, 47 and following; chapter 3:12.

### 538. **By what marks may one distinguish a true Church of Christ from a false one?**
Where the Spirit of Christ is given free mastery in all things; where, consequently, the Word of **God** is proclaimed pure and clear; and where the communal worship is established according to the same; where the love of Christ shines brightly, and the doctrine is adorned with a godly walk of life <note>original: *gottseligem Wandel*. This phrase describes a lifestyle that is visibly consistent with one’s religious devotion.</note>—there is a true Church of **God**. Acts 2:42. John 13:35. Philippians 1:27.

545. What do you think of the estate of authority <term>estate of authority: refers to the office and social standing of those who govern, such as magistrates or monarchs</term>?

It is the Law or Right of Nature <term>Law of Nature: moral principles believed to be inherent in human nature and discoverable by reason, serving as a foundation for man-made laws</term>, especially as it is clarified and explained in the Bible.

### 3. On Marriage.

553. **What then is marriage?**
Marriage <term>Marriage: The German "Ehestand" literally means "the marital state" or "estate of marriage," referring to it as a divinely ordained way of life.</term> is an indissoluble covenant between one man and one woman.

554. **Where does marriage come from?**
It was first established and instituted by God Himself, through both deed and word.

555. **When did this happen?**
When our first parents were still in Paradise, and in the state of innocence. <note>The "state of innocence" refers to the theological period before the "Fall of Man," when Adam and Eve were believed to be without sin.</note>

556. **How was it established through God’s deed?**
God took a rib from Adam and built <note>original: "bauete" — This follows the literal biblical German, suggesting the careful construction or fashioning of the first woman.</note> a woman from it, and led her to Adam, and joined them together. Genesis 2:21, 22; Mark 10:9.

557. **How was it established through God’s word?**
God the Lord said: "It is not good that man should be alone; I will make him a helper <term>helper: often translated elsewhere as "helpmeet," implying a partner who is a suitable counterpart.</term> to be with him." And afterward He said: "They shall be one flesh." Genesis 2:18, 24; Matthew 19:5.

564. What are the common duties of those joined in marriage toward one another?

These are all united in marital love, from which flows all faithfulness for the promotion of all welfare, both spiritual and bodily, toward one another until death parts them. Genesis 2:24; Ephesians 5:28, 29; 1 Corinthians 7:33, 34.

565. Did God not have something further in mind with the institution of marriage?

Yes, God intended to represent thereby an image of the mysterious union <note>original: "Bild der geheimen Verbindung." This refers to the concept of marriage as a "great mystery" (mysterium magnum) reflecting the bond between the divine and the human.</note> between Christ and His Church—indeed, with every believing soul. Ephesians 5:32.

566. What are we to note regarding marriage from this?

That this is a truly sanctified and Christian estate of marriage when both spouses stand in union with Christ through faith.

567. How may the estate of marriage be led in a Christian manner according to such a mystery?

On the part of the husband: that he follows that conduct toward his wife which Christ shows to His bride. Ephesians 5:23, 28, 29; Colossians 3:19; 1 Peter 3:7.

568. In what does this conduct primarily consist?

Christ loves, provides for, governs, and protects the Church <term>Church: The original "Gemeine" refers to the community of believers or the congregation.</term>, and bears patience with her weaknesses.

569. And how should the wife conduct herself in a Christian manner in this regard?
That she conducts herself toward her husband just as the <term>Church: The original German "Gemeine" refers to the community of believers or congregation.</term> conducts itself toward Christ.

She loves him heartily; she honors him and esteems him highly; she obeys him.

571. What kind of marriage is it that is conducted in such a way?
It is a holy and blessed marriage, which is never an obstacle to the Kingdom of God. Hebrews 13:4.

572. Based on all of this, what is one to think of the estate of marriage?
That it is a holy institution <note>original: "Stiftung." This implies something established or founded by divine decree.</note> given to the human race: but it can only be led in a Christian and God-pleasing manner through faith.

# VIII. On the Resurrection of the Dead.

573. How shall we consider the resurrection of the dead?
We will consider, first, death, and thereafter the resurrection.

574. What is death in itself?
Death is the <term>wages of sin: The original "Sünden-Sold" literally means the "pay" or "salary" earned by sin.</term>. A punishment and effect of sin. Romans 6:23.

<meta>catchword: 575. Whom</meta>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the eighth chapter on death and the resurrection, moving from the definition of death as a consequence of sin to the specific fates of the body and soul after life ends.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>On the Resurrection.</header>

### 575. Whom does death affect?
All people, because they have all sinned in Adam. Romans 5:12. Hebrews 9:27.

### 576. What is death’s actual work?
It destroys and annihilates the human physical form or body.

### 577. What follows this destruction?
The separation of the body and the soul.

### 578. What happens to the body after such separation?
It enters into <term>decay: The original German "Verwesung" refers to the natural process of decomposition.</term>, where it becomes earth again until the <term>Last Day: The day of final judgment and resurrection at the end of the world.</term>.

### 579. What happens to the soul after such separation?
It comes before its Judge, who assigns it to its place, where it awaits its reunion with the body. <note>original: "Pred. 12, 7." The book of "Prediger" is known as Ecclesiastes in English Bibles.</note> Ecclesiastes 12:7. 2 Corinthians 5:10.

### 580. Where are the souls of the unbelievers assigned?
They come to a place of torment and pain. Luke 16:23, 24.

### 581. Where, however, do the souls of the believers remain?
They come to rest with Christ their Lord. <note>original: "Weish. 3, 1." This refers to the Wisdom of Solomon, a book found in the Apocrypha.</note> Wisdom 3:1. Revelation 14:13. Hebrews 4:3. Philippians 1:23. Luke 23:43.

### 582. Who can die so blessedly?
Whoever perseveres (in faith and <term>godliness: The term "Gottseligkeit" describes a life defined by piety and a state of being "blessed in God."</term>) until the end shall be saved. Matthew 24:13. Revelation 2:10.

It distinguishes between the rest promised to believers and the torment assigned to unbelievers.</summary>
<keywords>death, soul, body, decay, Last Day, judgment, salvation, godliness, afterlife</keywords>

# IX. On the Last Judgment.

**597. What will follow after the resurrection of the dead?**
The <term>Last Judgment: In German, "Jüngstes Gericht," referring to the final and eternal judgment by God of all nations.</term>.

**598. Who will be the judge there?**
The very Son of God, Jesus Christ, who came into the flesh to redeem the world.
->John 5:22, 27.<-

**599. How will he come for the judgment?**
He will come quickly and unexpectedly, in his glory, and all the holy angels with him, and will sit upon the throne of his glory.
->Luke 21:35. Matthew 25:31; 16:27.<-

**600. What will then be made manifest in him?**
Both the majesty of his divine nature and the glory of his <term>transfigured: "Verklärt" in the original, meaning a body that has been glorified or made radiant after death.</term> human nature.

**601. Who will see him coming in this way?**
All people will see him coming in this way,

### 602. Who must appear before the judgment?
All who have ever fallen away from God, namely all fallen angels and all human beings.

Their state, whether they were believers or unbelievers. They must give an account of their thoughts, words, and works.
Mark 16:16. 1 Corinthians 4:5. Matthew 12:36, 37.

According to the yardstick of the Word of God, in divine <term>justice: In this context, "Gerechtigkeit" refers to God’s impartial and perfect judicial standard.</term>. John 12:48.
->Acts 17:31.<-

### 605. What do you mean by that?
The believers, whom Christ has redeemed from the curse of the Law, so that they are no longer under the Law but under grace, will be judged according to the <term>Gospel Covenant of Grace: "Evangelischen Gnaden-Bunde," the theological concept that salvation is a gift through faith in Christ rather than earned through following religious rules.</term>. Galatians 3:13. Romans 6:14–15.
Hebrews 10:16, 17; ch. 9:15. Romans 2:16.

### 606. By what will the unbelievers be judged?
The unbelievers will be condemned by the Law, under which they have remained; indeed, they are already judged, and the wrath of God remains upon them. Romans 2:12. John 3:18. Galatians 3:10.
John 3:36.

# X. On Eternal Life.

->629. What will happen next, after<-

### 4. How do you know that?
His dearest Son, our Lord Jesus Christ, has told us this many times. When we pray from the heart, his <term>Fatherly heart: "Vater-Herz," a term emphasizing God’s deep, parental compassion</term> is moved with mercy toward us. Luke 11:9, 13.

### 5. Do you also know how you should speak when you wish to pray, so that it may be well-pleasing to the Heavenly Father?
Yes, I know that, for my Lord Christ has taught me by <term>prescribing: "vorgesprochen," literally "spoken before," meaning providing a model to repeat</term> a form for us. Matthew 6:9, Luke 11:2.

### 6. Well then, that must be right: But tell me, how does this Lord’s Prayer, which he taught, go?
It goes as follows: Our Father who art in heaven. Hallowed be thy name. Thy kingdom come. Thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven. Give us this day our daily bread. And forgive us our debts, as we forgive our debtors. And lead us not into temptation; but deliver us from evil. For thine is the kingdom, and the power, and the glory forever, Amen.

### 8. What does devotion teach you about how this prayer might be divided?
I notice that it can be conveniently divided into three parts: first, the Entrance; second, the Prayer; third, the Conclusion.

### 9. How much belongs to the Entrance?
The Entrance comprises the words: **Our Father, who art in heaven.**

### 10. Whom do you take with you, then, when you say: **Our Father?**
I join myself to all the dear children of God who are still down here in this <term>valley of sorrows: "Jammerthal," a common Pietist and Baroque metaphor for earthly life, emphasizing its hardships and trials</term>, surrounded by all kinds of grief, misery, and poverty.

### 11. Why do you not do it for yourself alone, but also think of others at the same time?
I know the Father loves us all together; and we love one another as he has commanded us; therefore we turn in love, with one mind, to the Father. <margin>John 16:27, ch. 13:34.</margin>

### 12. Do you think that the rich God in heaven is the Father of such poor, afflicted people, and that he has a tender Fatherly love for them?
I believe that for certain, and that is also why I turn to him with prayer in my concerns, together with all his children, in childlike love and <term>confidence: "Zuversicht," a strong sense of trust and certain hope</term>. <margin>Isaiah 63:16, ch. 64:8.</margin>

In the first four petitions, we seek from the Father the granting of everything good; in the last three petitions, we persist in asking for the turning away of everything evil.

### 20. What is the Name of the Father?
It is all His divine majesty and glory, according to which He is the origin of all glorious power and the primal source of all good; indeed, He is the true Father over everything that is called a child. <note>This refers to the idea that all earthly fatherhood and family derive their name and essence from God. See Ephesians 3:15.</note>
Isaiah 42:8; 45:12. Hosea 12:6. Ephesians 3:15.

### 21. What is the second petition?
->**Thy kingdom come.**<-

### 22. What do we ask for in the second petition?
We pray for the coming of the <term>Kingdom of Grace: *Gnadenreich*; in Lutheran theology, this refers to God's spiritual reign on earth through the Gospel and the Holy Spirit</term>, that the fatherly government in the Holy Spirit would spread within us and over us all, so that the Church of God may be built up, but the kingdom of Satan be destroyed. Psalm 143:10; 25:4–5; 51:20. Romans 16:20.

### 23. What does the Kingdom of God bring with it when it comes?
It is, and it brings with it, righteousness, peace, and joy in the Holy Spirit. Romans 14:17.

### 24. What does the third petition say?
->**Thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven.**<-

### 25. What should we consider in the third petition?
We should consider that the Heavenly Father’s will alone is perfect, good, holy, and beneficial; however, our natural will is corrupt, perverse, evil, and harmful. <note>The contrast between God's "perfect will" and the "natural will" of fallen humanity is a central theme in Pietist devotion.</note> Romans 12:2.
1 Thessalonians 4:3. Genesis 6:3. Ephesians 2:3.

### 26. What do we now ask in this third petition?
We ask that the Father would grant that we all renounce our own will; and on the contrary, be surrendered and obedient to His good will, just as the angels in heaven do. <note>The renunciation of self-will (*Selbstverleugnung*) is a core theme in this period of German spirituality, emphasizing that human desires often conflict with divine purposes.</note> Luke 14:33.
->Psalm 141:4. Psalm 103:20–21.<-

### 27. What is the fourth petition?
->**Give us this day our daily bread.**<-

### 28. What do we confess by this?
We confess by this our constant need: that if our Heavenly Father did not daily provide us with all that we require for the preservation of both body and soul, we would surely perish. Psalm 145:15–16.

### 29. But for what kind of bread and nourishment do the children of God actually and primarily ask here?
The children of God seek first and foremost the spiritual <term>Heavenly Bread: *Himmelbrod*; a reference to both the Word of God and Jesus Christ himself (the "Bread of Life"), which sustains the spiritual life of the believer</term> that the Father gives, which belongs to their <term>New Being: *neuen Wesen*; the spiritual nature or "new man" created in a believer after rebirth</term>, without which they cannot live. Matthew 6:33. John 6:27, 32, 50, 53.

### 30. How does the fifth petition go?
->**And forgive us our debts, as we forgive our debtors.**<-

### 31. What is understood here by "debts"?
All sins and offenses are meant, by which we prove ourselves to be inconsistent with the Holy Will of God. <note>In German, the word *Schuld* carries the double meaning of "debt" and "guilt" or "sin." While English translations often use "trespasses," the author here links it directly to the failure to match God's will.</note> Luke 11:4. 1 John 3:4.

We ask for the forgiveness of the debts, for which 
we

->33. What is the sixth petition?<-
->**And lead us not into temptation.**<-

->34. What are we primarily to remember here?<-

<margin>1 Peter 5:8. James 1:14. Matthew 26:41.</margin>
That our sworn enemies <note>In the spiritual context of this period, "sworn enemies" or *abgesagte Feinde* typically refers to the traditional triad of the World, the Flesh, and the Devil.</note> pursue us incessantly with temptation; and that we are, of ourselves, so weak that we cannot withstand them for a single moment.

->35. What, therefore, do we ask for in this sixth petition?<-

<margin>1 Thessalonians 3:13. Ephesians 3:14, 16. Chapter 6:10–13.</margin>
We ask that the Heavenly Father, in such danger, would not abandon us to the temptations of our enemies, but would graciously strengthen, assist, and preserve us.

->36. Now, how does the seventh and final petition read?<-

->**But deliver us from evil.**<-

->37. What kind of evil is meant here?<-

<margin>Revelation 12:9.</margin>
All evil is meant—whatever is an evil and a wickedness in our eyes and in God’s eyes—primarily <term>Satan: derived from the Hebrew word for "Accuser" or "Adversary"</term>, along with all the evil and harm that he has established and still brings about within and outside of mankind.

->38. What do we ask regarding such evil?<-

We ask that the dear Heavenly Father would deliver and... <note>The text breaks off here to be continued on the next page; the original German catchword "befreyen" indicates the sentence concludes with "set us free" or "deliver us."</note>

<page-num>128</page-num>
<header>On the Lord’s Prayer.</header>

liberate us <note>original: "befreyen"; this completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the seventh petition</note>, so that we might serve Him in holiness and truth. Luke 1:74, 75.

### 39. What does the conclusion finally contain?

At the conclusion we say: **For Thine is the kingdom and the power and the glory forever, Amen.**

### 40. What do we indicate with these concluding words?

We indicate to the Father how we find it most fitting <note>original: "billig"; in this historical context, this means "appropriate" or "rightfully due," rather than "inexpensive"</note> that we approach Him specifically, with childlike trust, with these our petitions, and that we wish to turn nowhere else.

### 41. What reason do we provide for this?

We bear witness that indeed the kingdom for which we have prayed is His; that all the power necessary for us is His; and that therefore all honor and praise is due and belongs to Him; and all this continually, without beginning or end.

### 42. Why do we say at the very end, Amen?

Amen means: **Yes, certainly, truly.** <note>The word "Amen" is a transliteration of a Hebrew term meaning "so be it" or "truth."</note> As if we were saying: **Father, what we have confessed and prayed for comes from our hearts, so that it certainly and truly be so and come to pass.**

<keywords>Lord's Prayer, Doxology, Kingdom of God, Amen, prayer, childlike trust, divine power, glory, Luke 1</keywords>

# Mirror of Life

-> drawn from <-

## the Holy Ten Commandments,

-> arranged in <-

### Question and Answer format.

1. What rule of life <term>Lebens-Regel: a standard or guideline for conduct</term> did God first establish for fallen humanity?
The first established rule of life was revealed and given by God through Moses, His servant, in His Moral Law <term>Sittliches Gesetze: the ethical or moral laws governing human conduct, distinct from ritual or civil laws</term>.

2. How is the Law of Moses divided?
It is divided into the Ceremonial Law <term>Ceremonialische Gesetze: laws concerning religious rituals, sacrifices, and Jewish temple practices</term> and the Moral Law.

3. What is the Ceremonial Law?
The Ceremonial Law comprises and contains within it all the commandments which direct and describe the Jewish religion and divine service <note>original: "Gottesdienst"</note>.

4. What does the Moral Law contain?
The Moral Law is summarized <note>original: "summarisch begriffen"</note> within the Holy Ten Commandments.

5. What is the essence <note>original: "Summa"</note> of the Ten Commandments?
The essence of the Ten Commandments consists in this:

# Of the Holy Ten Commandments.
<meta>catchword: letter</meta>

Inwardly: You shall love **God** your **Lord**, with all your heart, with all your soul, with all your strength, and with all your mind, and your neighbor as yourself. <note>This "inner" summary is the Great Commandment from Luke 10:27, used here to summarize the spirit of the Law.</note>

->6. How are the Ten Commandments<-
->divided?<-

**God** has distinguished them into two tables: The commandments of the first table pertain to **God** the **Lord**, and the commandments of the other table concern one's neighbor.

->7. How many commandments does each table have?<-

The first table contains four commandments, and the other table contains six commandments. <note>The author follows the Lutheran and Catholic tradition of grouping the commandments, which assigns four to the first table (duties to God) and six to the second (duties to others).</note>

->8. Why did **God** divide them in this<-
->way, and distinguish them into two<-
->tables?<-

So that we should properly notice how we are to practice and conduct ourselves in love toward **God**, and in love toward our neighbor.

->9. How are the Ten Commandments to<-
->be understood?<-

They have their manifest, literal meaning <term>Verstand: here meaning the "sense" or "intellectual grasp" of the words</term>: however, a hidden spiritual sense lies concealed beneath it, which is what **God** truly intended.

->10. How do you know this?<-

Christ our **Lord** taught it to us, in that He Himself in the Sermon on the Mount interpreted several of the Ten Commandments according to that hidden spiritual sense.

->11. What is the literal meaning?<-

It is the actual meaning as the let- <note>The text breaks here mid-word; the original German "Buch-" is the start of "Buchstabe" or "letter," referring to the "letter of the law."</note>

# Of the Holy Ten Commandments.

...ter <note>This completes the word "letter" from the previous page.</note> sounds; and is called the moral teaching; wherein the outward actions and works of the body are contained, by which one can do right or wrong.

### 12. What is the spiritual sense?

It is that which is indicated in a spiritual manner beneath the letter, and which concerns the desires, internal cravings <term>Lüste: in this context, refers to any disordered or strong worldly desires, not exclusively sexual lust</term>, and impulses of the soul.

### 13. What does the literal understanding command?

It commands all virtuous conduct toward **GOD** and neighbor.

### 14. What does the spiritual sense command?

It commands all virtues, and specifically that they should flow from a sincere, loving heart and be practiced accordingly.

### 15. What does the literal understanding forbid?

All such vices, sins, and misdeeds which can also, to some extent, be recognized from the law of nature <term>Gesetz der Natur: the idea that certain moral truths are "written on the heart" and knowable through human reason alone</term>; and which run contrary to all reasonable honor and natural justice.

### 16. What does the spiritual sense forbid?

All sin and transgression that happens through cravings and desires in the soul; as well as whatever sinful things are carried out in the reason through thoughts, or likewise with the external senses, such as hearing, seeing, and speaking.

<meta>Catchword: 17. Are</meta>

<summary>This page defines the difference between the literal and spiritual interpretations of the Ten Commandments, emphasizing that while the literal sense governs outward actions and natural justice, the spiritual sense governs the internal desires and thoughts of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Ten Commandments, literal sense, spiritual sense, moral teaching, Law of Nature, internal desires, virtue, vice, reason, soul</keywords>

# Of the Holy Ten Commandments.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

### 17. Are the Ten Commandments, according to both the literal and spiritual sense, still to be seen and kept today as a rule of life?

Yes, the Ten Commandments are also given to us by **GOD**, so that they should be an external rule <note>original: "Richtschnur," meaning a plumb line or guiding principle used to ensure a building is straight</note> for our conduct and life, understood in such a double sense.

<note>The conscience is the internal rule.</note>

### 18. With what do you prove this?

> Christ says: Truly I say to you, until heaven and earth pass away, not the smallest letter, nor a tittle of the law shall pass away.

->Matthew 5, 18.<-

### 19. What then is briefly comprised in the commandments of the first table?

In the first commandment of the first table <note>The "First Table" traditionally refers to the commandments regarding our relationship with God</note>, <term>idolatry: the worship of false gods or putting anything before God</term> is forbidden; in the second, false worship; in the third, false doctrine; in the fourth, pure and true worship is commanded.

### 20. What is briefly comprised in the commandments of the second table?

In the first commandment of the second table <note>The "Second Table" refers to the commandments regarding our relationship with our neighbors</note>, obedience and submission are commanded; in the second, anger and manslaughter are forbidden; in the third, unchastity, fornication, and adultery; in the fourth, unfaithfulness and theft; in the fifth, lies and untruth; in the sixth, all desire that is blameworthy and sinful before **GOD**.

### 21. How does the introduction to the Ten Commandments read?

# Of the Holy Ten Commandments.

The introduction to the Ten Commandments reads as follows: I am the **LORD** your **GOD**, who brought you out of Egypt, out of the house of bondage <note>original: "Dienst-Hause," literally "service-house," traditionally translated in English Bibles as "house of bondage"</note>.

### 22. What does this introduction teach us?
It teaches us that, because **GOD** is the **LORD**, and is our **GOD** and our Savior, we are therefore bound to keep all His commandments.

### 23. How does the first commandment read?
You shall have no other gods before me.

### 24. What is forbidden in the first commandment?
In the first commandment, all <term>idolatry: the worship of idols or placing created things above the Creator</term> and falling away from the only true **GOD** is forbidden; likewise all unbelief and mistrust toward Him, and that one should honor and worship no other god except Him alone.

### 25. What is commanded in the first commandment?
That we should heartily fear and love **GOD**, so that indeed before His all-seeing eyes and holy face, we recognize and confess no other but Him alone as our **GOD** and Savior; and that in all our bodily and spiritual distress, we direct both our heart and trust toward Him alone.

### 26. How does the second commandment read? <note>The numbering here follows the Reformed tradition, which treats the prohibition of images as a distinct second commandment, whereas the Lutheran and Catholic traditions typically group it with the first.</note>
You shall make for yourself no image <note>original: "Bildniß"</note>, nor any likeness. Do not worship them, and do not serve them.

### 27. What is forbidden in the second commandment?
In the second commandment is forbidden all <term>image-service: the religious veneration or worship of physical representations of the divine</term> and worship; where one does not [worship] **GOD** according to His... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword on the original page likely leads to "word" or "command" on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page begins a detailed catechetical study of the Ten Commandments, focusing on the introduction and the first two commandments regarding the exclusive worship of God and the prohibition of idols.</summary>
<keywords>Ten Commandments, Decalogue, Idolatry, Exodus, First Commandment, Second Commandment, Worship, Catechism</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed catechism on the Ten Commandments, beginning with the conclusion of the Second Commandment (regarding the prohibition of idols) and covering the Third and Fourth Commandments. The numbering continues to follow the Reformed tradition, which distinguishes the prohibition of images from the first commandment.</meta>

[...contrary to] His command—but rather, wishes to serve Him through images and <term>creatures: in this context, any created thing or being, as opposed to the Creator</term>; and one thereby makes them partakers of divine honor: concerning which **GOD** is a jealous **GOD**, and will not give His honor to another.

### 28. What is commanded in the second commandment?

That we should offer all our divine service, both inwardly and outwardly, in a sincere, complete, and pure manner, to the only true, living **GOD** alone, and not bestow any divine honor upon any thing besides Him, whatever it may be, under penalty of divine <note>original: "Eifer," which in the 18th century German Bible refers to God's "jealousy" or "zeal" to protect His holiness (Exodus 20:5)</note> jealousy.

### 29. How does the third commandment read?

> You shall not misuse the name of the **LORD** your **GOD**.

### 30. What is forbidden in the third commandment?

In the third commandment, it is forbidden to blaspheme and misuse the most holy name of **GOD** with cursing, scolding, swearing, and <term>sorcery: the superstitious or magical use of holy names or symbols</term>; likewise, to use, name, or cite it in a frivolous, useless, or worldly manner, or in a lighthearted way.

### 31. What is commanded in the third commandment?

That we should use the holy name of **GOD** in all humility, fear, and great reverence: so that He may be rightly confessed, called upon, and praised by us in all our words and works.

### 32. How does the fourth commandment read?

> You shall keep the <term>holy day: the Sabbath or day of rest, traditionally Sunday in the Christian context</note> holy.

On the Holy Ten Commandments? 135

### 33. What is forbidden in the fourth commandment?

In the fourth commandment is forbidden all vanity and neglect, or the frivolous spending of <term>divine duties: 'Gottesdienstlichen Pflichten', referring to the obligations of formal worship and religious service</term> on the days and times set and appointed for worship: such as when those times are spent with unnecessary thoughts, words, or works concerning worldly business and <term>amusements: 'Erlustigung', meaning recreational pleasures or entertainment that distract from spiritual focus</term>.

### 34. What is commanded in the fourth commandment?

That we should spend the appointed times of worship not with worldly business, but in holy devotion, with godly practice, both publicly and privately; and let **GOD** rest and work in our hearts.

### 35. How does the fifth commandment read?

You shall honor your father and your mother, that it may go well with you and that you may live long upon the earth.

### 36. What is forbidden in the fifth commandment?

In the fifth commandment is forbidden all disobedience, <term>rebelliousness: 'Widerspenstigkeit', a stubborn or contrary spirit against authority</term>, and dishonor toward parents and all other <term>superiors and authorities: 'Vorgesetzten und Oberkeiten', which includes both household heads and civil government officials</term>; all rebelling and resisting, in deeds and words, against their command and will.

### 37. What is commanded in the fifth commandment?

That we should show all honor, love, and loyalty to father and mother and all who are placed over us; that we should obediently submit ourselves to all their good teaching and <term>discipline: 'Strafe', which in this historical context refers to corrective guidance or parental punishment intended for the child's benefit</term>, and that we should have patience with their shortcomings <note>The text emphasizes that parental authority is to be respected even when the parents themselves are flawed or "feeble" in character.</note>.

Of the Holy Ten Commandments. 136

### 38. How does the sixth commandment read?
You shall not kill.

### 39. What is forbidden in the sixth commandment?
In the sixth commandment is forbidden all killing and murder, whether of oneself or of any <term>fellow human: 'Neben-Menschens', literally those who live alongside us</term>; all brawling, striking, and fighting; all anger, envy, thirst for revenge, <term>resentment: 'Mißgunst', the feeling of begrudging another's success or happiness</term>, and suspicion toward one's neighbor.

### 40. What is commanded in the sixth commandment?
That we should love our neighbor; that we should show toward him all patience, peace, gentleness, mercy, and friendliness, and avert any harm from him as much as lies within us, for the preservation and welfare of his life.

### 41. How does the seventh commandment read?
You shall not commit adultery.

### 42. What is forbidden in the seventh commandment?
In the seventh commandment is forbidden all <term>fornication: 'Unzucht', referring generally to sexual immorality or lewdness</term>, <term>profligacy: 'Hurerey', an archaic term for sexual vice</term>, adultery, and unchastity, along with all unchaste deeds, words, thoughts, gestures, lusts, and whatever may entice a person toward them.

### 43. What is commanded in the seventh commandment?
That we should have a chaste, pure, and <term>modest: 'züchtiges', implying a disciplined and decent character</term> heart; and also that we should preserve our body, life, and senses in pure chastity, and keep ourselves clean from all impure defilement of both body and soul.

### 44. How does the eighth commandment read?
You shall not steal.

### 45. What is forbidden in the eighth commandment?
In the eighth commandment is forbidden all <term>dishonesty: 'Untreu', referring to a breach of trust or lack of integrity in dealings</term>, robbery, and theft, and all fraud, trickery, and <term>schemes: 'Anschläge', meaning calculated plots or designs to gain something</term> to bring the neighbor’s property to oneself, along with all injustice.

### 46. What is commanded in the eighth commandment?
That we should demonstrate all faithfulness and sincerity toward our neighbor; heartily wish them well in all that is theirs, and seek to promote their benefit.

### 47. How does the ninth commandment read?
You shall not bear false witness against your neighbor.

### 48. What is forbidden in the ninth commandment?
In the ninth commandment is forbidden all untruth, lies, and false testimony against the neighbor; likewise all <term>backbiting: 'Afterreden', literally speaking 'behind' someone or speaking ill of them in their absence</term>, twisting of words, slander, and evil gossip, through which the neighbor’s good name, honor, and <term>reputation: 'Glimpf', an old term for one's good standing or propriety</term> are diminished.

### 49. What is commanded in the ninth commandment?
That we should heartily love and promote the truth in all things, and for the neighbor's sake, gladly defend his honor and name through truthful testimony wherever it is necessary.

### 50. How does the tenth commandment read?
You shall not covet your neighbor's house,

# Devotion 2c.

->5.<-

O grant that we may embrace the pure doctrine <term>pure doctrine<gloss>A term often used in post-Reformation texts to refer to biblical teaching that is considered free from human error or unauthorized church traditions.</gloss></term> with true faith, and that we do not deviate from it or give it up for the sake of human delusions.

->6.<-

O faithful Master! Stand by us with the gifts of your Spirit, so that we may always have grace, peace, and joy through our knowledge of you.

->7.<-

So that we too may heartily bring you praise, fame, and honor, until we are ready to sing of your glory and honor throughout eternity. Amen.

->GLORY TO GOD ALONE!<- <note>original: "SOLI DEO GLORIA!"</note>

->T H E  E N D.<-
